## Getting Started

### Sounds and tones (`gs-1`)
Sources consulted: Needs cross-check against a published Yoruba orthography guide before verifying the letter sounds.; Wiktionary, "ọkọ" (Yoruba section): ọkọ husband, ọkọ̀ vehicle.; Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", Phonology section: acute for high, grave for low, mid unmarked.; The read-aloud words ẹ̀gbọ́n, iṣẹ́, ṣíbí, omi, ilé, bàbá and ìyá were added for practice. Their tone marks are standard spellings but were not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `omo`: **ọmọ** (child)
  - Parts: ọ + mọ
  - Doubt: Syllable glosses are pronunciation guides, not meanings. Confirm the English approximations.

  - Parts: ẹ + ja
  - Doubt: Confirm the English approximation of ẹ.

  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives mid-mid for husband. Wiktionary also lists "dear, beloved" as a second sense. Confirm the tones with a speaker.

  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives mid-low. Confirm that "vehicle" covers both car and boat, as Wiktionary suggests.

  - Doubt: Confirm the tone on gbọ́.

### Greetings and your name (`gs-2`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 1, Lesson 1 "Ìkíni", page 32: kú + àárọ̀ = káàárọ̀, kú + alẹ́ = káalẹ́; ẹ used to greet an older person, and káàárọ̀ o with no ẹ as the reply to a friend or younger sibling. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Wiktionary, "káàárọ̀" and "káalẹ́" (Yoruba sections): etymology kú + noun, and the usage note that ẹ comes first when speaking respectfully.; Wiktionary, "mi" and "rẹ" (Yoruba sections): my and your, placed after the noun.; The sentence Orúkọ mi ni Bùkọ́lá is built from the noun + mi pattern and ni. It was written for this lesson and not found written out in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.; The extra greetings (káàsán, ẹ kú ìrọ̀lẹ́, ẹ kú àbọ̀, ẹ kú iṣẹ́, ẹ ṣé, ó dàárọ̀, Báwo ni, Mo wà dáadáa) are common Yoruba phrases written out for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `e-kaaro`: **ẹ káàárọ̀** (good morning (to an elder or a group))
  - Parts: ẹ + kú + àárọ̀
  - Steps: ẹ kúàárọ̀ > ẹ káàárọ̀
  - Doubt: Sources show kú + àárọ̀ = káàárọ̀, with the high tone of kú kept. Describing this as the u turning into a is an interpretation. Sources do not gloss kú here; "greeting word" is a placeholder gloss.
- `e-kaale`: **ẹ káalẹ́** (good evening (to an elder or a group))
  - Parts: ẹ + kú + alẹ́
  - Steps: ẹ kúalẹ́ > ẹ káalẹ́
  - Doubt: COERLL and Wiktionary both give káalẹ́. Confirm the mid tone on the second a, and whether a shorter form with the u dropped is also used.
- `oruko-mi`: **Orúkọ mi ni Bùkọ́lá** (My name is Bùkọ́lá)
  - Parts: Orúkọ + mi + ni + Bùkọ́lá
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson from the noun + mi pattern. Confirm the tones of orúkọ and that ni is the natural linker here.
- `ki-ni-oruko-re`: **Kí ni orúkọ rẹ** (What is your name?)
  - Parts: Kí ni + orúkọ + rẹ
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones of orúkọ and kí ni. Beginner 4 teaches kí ni questions in full.

### Where you are from (`gs-3`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "wá" and "láti" (Yoruba sections): wá to come, láti from.; The sentences Mo wá láti Nàìjíríà, Ó wá láti Amẹ́ríkà and Wọ́n wá láti Èkó, and the question Níbo ni o ti wá, were written for this lesson. Not found written out in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.; The questions Orílẹ̀-èdè wo ni o ti wá, Ìlú wo ni o ti wá, Abúlé wo ni o ti wá and Ìpínlẹ̀ wo ni o ti wá, and the answers Orílẹ̀-èdè mi ni Nàìjíríà, Ìlú mi ni Ìbàdàn and Ìpínlẹ̀ mi ni Ọ̀yọ́, were written for this lesson from the noun + wo (which) and noun + mi + ni patterns. Not found written out in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `mo-wa`: **Mo wá láti Nàìjíríà** (I come from Nigeria)
  - Parts: Mo + wá + láti + Nàìjíríà
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson. Confirm wá láti is the natural way to say where you come from, and the tones of Nàìjíríà.
- `o-he-wa`: **Ó wá láti Amẹ́ríkà** (He or she comes from America)
  - Parts: Ó + wá + láti + Amẹ́ríkà
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones of Amẹ́ríkà. COERLL and Wiktionary agree on the high tone of ó (he, she, it).
- `won-wa`: **Wọ́n wá láti Èkó** (They come from Lagos)
  - Parts: Wọ́n + wá + láti + Èkó
  - Doubt: Confirm Èkó as the Yoruba name for Lagos and its tones. wọn without a mark is a different form (them).
- `nibo-ni-o-wa`: **Níbo ni o ti wá** (Where are you from?)
  - Parts: Níbo + ni + o + ti + wá
  - Doubt: Learn this as a fixed phrase for now. ni and ti are taught properly later. Confirm the phrase and its tones.
- `ilu-wo`: **Ìlú wo ni o ti wá** (Which town are you from?)
  - Parts: Ìlú + wo + ni + o + ti wá
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson on the pattern of Níbo ni o ti wá. Confirm wo after the noun and the tones.
- `ilu-mi`: **Ìlú mi ni Ìbàdàn** (My town is Ibadan)
  - Parts: Ìlú + mi + ni + Ìbàdàn
  - Doubt: Same pattern as Orúkọ mi ni Bùkọ́lá in the previous lesson. Confirm the tones of Ìbàdàn and that this is the natural reply.

## Beginner

### "To be": jẹ́ and ni (`b-1`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 5, Lesson 1 "The verbs jẹ́ and ni", pages 115 to 116: jẹ́ with professions (Mo jẹ́ akẹ́kọ̀ọ́, I am a student); ni in Èmi ni màmáa Túndé and Dókítà ni Ládi; *Mo ni dókítà and *Ó ni olùkọ́ marked incorrect because regular pronouns cannot be used with ni. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 36: Àwa jẹ́ ọ̀rẹ́ (we are friends), given as an exercise sentence.; Wiktionary, "jẹ" (Yoruba section): jẹ́, high tone, to be; jẹ, mid tone, to eat.; Wiktionary, "ni" (Yoruba section): ni, mid tone, to be (identification); usage note that ni cannot be used with regular subject pronouns such as mo or ó, with the example Òun ni ọ̀rẹ́ mi (he is my friend). ní, high tone, is a different word (to have; at).; Wiktionary, "olukọ" (Yoruba section): olùkọ́ teacher, with a quoted sentence ẹ jẹ́ olùkọ́ from Schleicher, Jẹ́ K'Á Sọ Yorùbá (Yale University Press, 1993), page 257.; Wiktionary, "eyi", "ile", "ọrẹ", "mi" (Yoruba sections): èyí this, ilé house, ọ̀rẹ́ friend, mi my.; The extra jobs (dókítà, nọ́ọ̀sì, àgbẹ̀, awakọ̀, oníṣòwò, akọrin, alásè), Kí ni iṣẹ́ rẹ and Iṣẹ́ mi ni olùkọ́ were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `mo-je-oluko`: **Mo jẹ́ olùkọ́** (I am a teacher)
  - Parts: Mo + jẹ́ + olùkọ́
  - Doubt: COERLL gives the same pattern with akẹ́kọ̀ọ́ (Mo jẹ́ akẹ́kọ̀ọ́, I am a student). The sentence with olùkọ́ is built on that pattern. Confirm the tones on olùkọ́.
- `awa-je-ore`: **Àwa jẹ́ ọ̀rẹ́** (We are friends)
  - Parts: Àwa + jẹ́ + ọ̀rẹ́
  - Doubt: Appears in COERLL as an exercise sentence without an English gloss. Confirm the gloss and the tones on ọ̀rẹ́ (low then high).
- `oun-ni-ore-mi`: **Òun ni ọ̀rẹ́ mi** (He (or she) is my friend)
  - Parts: Òun + ni + ọ̀rẹ́ + mi
  - Doubt: Taken from the Wiktionary usage note on ni. COERLL writes a longer vowel before mi in some phrases (for example olùkọ́ọ̀ mi); this lesson writes the plain form. Confirm.
- `mo-je-dokita`: **Mo jẹ́ dókítà** (I am a doctor)
  - Parts: Mo + jẹ́ + dókítà
  - Doubt: Same pattern as Mo jẹ́ olùkọ́. Confirm the tones of dókítà.
- `ise-mi-ni`: **Iṣẹ́ mi ni olùkọ́** (My job is teacher)
  - Parts: Iṣẹ́ + mi + ni + olùkọ́
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson on the pattern of Orúkọ mi ni Bùkọ́lá. Confirm this is the natural reply to Kí ni iṣẹ́ rẹ.

### Negation with kò (`b-2`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 1, Lesson 2 "Negation of verbs using kò", page 35: kò goes before the verb; N kò fẹ́ jẹ búrẹ́dì (I do not want to eat bread). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76: pronoun table with kò, including N kò (I), A kò (we), and Kò with no pronoun for he or she.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 4, Lesson 2 dialogue: O ò ṣe kúkú... (the short form ò after the pronoun o).; Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (Yoruba sections): kò, low tone, not, placed before a verb; ò is a short form of kò, frequently used after personal pronouns.; Wiktionary, "n" (Yoruba section): n as a form of mo (I), used in negative or future sentences.; Wiktionary, "mi" (Yoruba section): mi listed as a Lagos form of mo (I). Wiktionary, "ṣe": example Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan (I don't want to do anything).; Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (aspect and negation tables citing Adebayo 2021 and Ogunbọwale 1970): kò negates the plain verb; mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé means "I went to school".; COERLL translates kò + verb as "is not ...-ing" in its page 35 examples, while the Wiktionary table pairs kò with the plain past. This lesson uses "did not" and notes the other reading. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra verbs (wá, mu, rí, ra, kà, sùn, mọ̀), things (búrẹ́dì, ìwé) and the sentences N kò jẹ ẹja and A ò mu omi were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `ko-lo`: **Kò lọ** (He (or she) did not go)
  - Parts: Kò + lọ
  - Doubt: COERLL's table shows Kò with no pronoun for he or she. Confirm whether Kò lọ is heard as "did not go", "is not going", or both.
- `n-ko-lo`: **N kò lọ** (I did not go)
  - Parts: N + kò + lọ
  - Doubt: COERLL writes N kò and Wiktionary lists n as the form of mo in negative sentences. Neither marks a tone on n. Confirm the tone.
- `a-o-lo`: **A ò lọ** (We did not go)
  - Parts: A + kò + lọ
  - Steps: A kò lọ > A ò lọ
  - Doubt: COERLL gives A kò. Wiktionary says ò is frequent after pronouns but does not write A ò lọ itself. Confirm that the short form keeps the low tone.
- `mi-o-lo`: **Mi ò lọ** (I did not go (everyday form))
  - Parts: Mi + ò + lọ
  - Doubt: Wiktionary shows Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan and labels mi for I as a Lagos form. No consulted source gives the full form Mi kò, so no step is shown. Confirm how widely Mi ò is used.
- `n-ko-je-eja`: **N kò jẹ ẹja** (I did not eat fish)
  - Parts: N + kò + jẹ + ẹja
  - Doubt: Built on N kò lọ and the COERLL pattern N kò fẹ́ jẹ búrẹ́dì. Confirm the tones; jẹ (eat) is mid, not the high-tone jẹ́.
- `a-o-mu-omi`: **A ò mu omi** (We did not drink water)
  - Parts: A + ò + mu + omi
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson on the pattern of A ò lọ. Confirm the tones of mu and omi.

### Ongoing action with ń (`b-3`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 1, Lesson 3 "The progressive marker ń", page 38: ń goes before the verb for an ongoing action, like English -ing; without it the verb means past (Mò ń lọ, I am going; Mo lọ, I went). Page 37: Òun ń ṣeré / Ó ń ṣeré (he or she is playing). Page 33: Adé ń mu omi (Adé is drinking water). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Wiktionary, "n" (Yoruba section): ń marks the progressive, with the example Mo ń jẹun (I am eating; I was eating).; Wiktionary, "jẹun" (Yoruba section): jẹun, to eat food, from jẹ (to eat) + ohun (thing).; Wiktionary, "jẹ" and "ounjẹ" (Yoruba sections): jẹ, mid tone, to eat; oúnjẹ (food) comes from ohun + jíjẹ.; COERLL writes the pronoun as mò (low tone) before ń, while Wiktionary writes Mo ń jẹun. This lesson follows Wiktionary and notes the difference. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra verbs (kọrin, jó, rìn, sáré, ṣeré, kàwé, ṣiṣẹ́, sọ̀rọ̀) and the sentences A ń kọrin and Kí ni o ń ṣe were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `mo-n-jeun`: **Mo ń jẹun** (I am eating)
  - Parts: Mo + ń + jẹun
  - Doubt: Wiktionary writes Mo ń jẹun. COERLL writes the pronoun as mò, with a low mark, before ń (Mò ń lọ). Confirm which spelling to teach.
- `jeun`: **jẹun** (eat (a meal))
  - Parts: jẹ + ohun
  - Steps: jẹohun > jẹun
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives jẹ + ohun as the origin, and this rests on that one dictionary entry. It does not describe the sound changes, so the single drop step is a simplification. Confirm the tones of ohun.
- `o-n-lo`: **Ó ń lọ** (He (or she) is going)
  - Parts: Ó + ń + lọ
  - Doubt: Built on COERLL's Ó ń ṣeré (he or she is playing) and Mò ń lọ (I am going). Ó ń lọ itself is not written out there. Confirm.
- `ade-n-mu-omi`: **Adé ń mu omi** (Adé is drinking water)
  - Parts: Adé + ń + mu + omi
  - Doubt: Taken from COERLL page 33. Confirm the mid tones on mu and omi.
- `a-n-kori`: **A ń kọrin** (We are singing)
  - Parts: A + ń + kọrin
  - Doubt: Written for this lesson on the pattern of Mo ń jẹun. Confirm the tones of kọrin.
- `ki-ni-o-n-se`: **Kí ni o ń ṣe** (What are you doing?)
  - Parts: Kí ni + o + ń + ṣe
  - Doubt: A common question, built from kí ni, o, ń and ṣe. Confirm the tones and word order.

### Questions: ṣé and kí ni (`b-4`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 1, Lesson 4 "Interrogatives Kí ni? and Ṣé?", page 41: Kí ni o fẹ́? (what do you want?), Kí ni orúkọ rẹ? (what is your name?, written there with a longer vowel, orúkọọ̀ rẹ), Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun? (do you want to eat?). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27: Ṣé o sùn dáadáa? asked by a father to his son in the morning.; Wiktionary, "ṣe" (Yoruba section): ṣé, high tone, a particle placed before a sentence to make it a yes or no question; ṣe, mid tone, is the verb "do".; Wiktionary, "daadaa" (Yoruba section): dáadáa, well, with the example Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa? (did you sleep well?).; Wiktionary, "orukọ" and "rẹ" (Yoruba sections): orúkọ name; the phrase kí ni orúkọ rẹ; rẹ (mid tone) your, rẹ̀ (low tone) his or her.; Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", Grammar section: kí ni asks "what?" about things, ta ni asks "who?" about people.; Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra question words (ta ni, báwo, mélòó, nígbà wo), the answers Bẹ́ẹ̀ ni and Rárá, and the sentences Ṣé o fẹ́ omi and Ṣé o ń jẹun were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `ki-ni-o-fe`: **Kí ni o fẹ́** (What do you want?)
  - Parts: Kí + ni + o + fẹ́

## Intermediate

### Done, will, and usually: ti, máa, yóò (`i-1`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 3, Lesson 2 "Future Tense Máa", page 76: Mo máa lọ (I will go); máa works with both short and long pronouns. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 9, Lesson 2, page 219: exercise sentences with ti, including Olú ti jẹun and Mo ti lọ sí ibẹ̀.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 2, Lesson 2, page 55: Àwọn ọkùnrin máa ń dé fìlà (men do wear hats). Chapter 4, Lesson 2 exercise: Aago mélòó ni o máa ń jẹ oúnjẹ ọ̀sán? (asking at what time you eat lunch).; Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (citing Ajani 2001, Bamgboṣe 2000, Ogunbọwale 1970): ti marks an action that has happened and still matters (ẹ ti jẹun, you have eaten); máa marks an expected future; yóò marks an intended future; máa ń marks a habit; a pronoun before yóò must be a long form.; Wiktionary, "ti", "maa", "yoo" (Yoruba sections): Mo ti ṣe é tán (I have completed it); Mo máa lọ sí Èkó (I'll be going to Lagos); yóò, will.; Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The time words (lónìí, lánàá, lọ́la, nísinsìnyí, ní àárọ̀, ní alẹ́, lójoojúmọ́, nígbà gbogbo) and the sentences Mo máa wá lọ́la, Èmi yóò ra ìwé lónìí and Mo máa ń jẹun ní àárọ̀ were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `mo-ti-jeun`: **Mo ti jẹun** (I have eaten)
  - Parts: Mo + ti + jẹun
  - Doubt: COERLL has Olú ti jẹun and Wiktionary has ẹ ti jẹun. Wiktionary adds that ti can also be read as "ate". Confirm the mid tone on ti.
- `mo-maa-lo`: **Mo máa lọ** (I will go)
  - Parts: Mo + máa + lọ
  - Doubt: Taken from COERLL page 76. COERLL also lists Mà á lọ for I will go. Confirm the tones on máa (high, mid).
- `emi-yoo-lo`: **Èmi yóò lọ** (I will go (I intend to))
  - Parts: Èmi + yóò + lọ
  - Doubt: Built from the Wiktionary rule that yóò takes a long pronoun, and its example Èmi yóò máa lọ. Not found in COERLL. Wiktionary lists yóó, yíò, and yíó as other spellings. Confirm the tones.
- `mo-maa-n-jeun`: **Mo máa ń jẹun** (I usually eat)
  - Parts: Mo + máa + ń + jẹun
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists máa ń as the habit marker, and COERLL uses o máa ń jẹ oúnjẹ ọ̀sán for a daily meal. Mo máa ń jẹun itself is not written out in a source. Confirm the English "usually".
- `mo-maa-wa-lola`: **Mo máa wá lọ́la** (I will come tomorrow)
  - Parts: Mo + máa + wá + lọ́la
  - Doubt: Built on Mo máa lọ and the Wiktionary entry for ọ̀la. Confirm the tones on lọ́la.
- `emi-yoo-ra-iwe`: **Èmi yóò ra ìwé lónìí** (I will buy a book today)
  - Parts: Èmi + yóò + ra + ìwé + lónìí
  - Doubt: Built on Èmi yóò lọ. Confirm the tones on lónìí and the place of the time word.
- `mo-maa-n-jeun-aaro`: **Mo máa ń jẹun ní àárọ̀** (I usually eat in the morning)
  - Parts: Mo + máa ń + jẹun + ní àárọ̀
  - Doubt: Built on Mo máa ń jẹun. Confirm ní àárọ̀ and its place at the end.

### Possession: the owner comes second (`i-2`)
Sources consulted: Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", Grammar section (citing Bamgboṣe 1966 and Rowlands 1969): possession is shown by putting two nouns side by side, the thing first and the owner second, as in fìlà Àkàndé (Àkàndé's cap) and inú àpótí (the inside of the box).; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 2, Lesson 1 "Possessive Pronouns", page 51: mi (my), rẹ (your), rẹ̀ (his, her), wa (our), yín (your, plural), wọn (their), written after the noun with a lengthened vowel, as in ìwéè mi (my book) and ilée wọn (their house). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 1, page 29 dialogue: Orúkọ bàbáà mi ni Kúnlé Akínlàjà (my father's name is Kúnlé Akínlàjà).; Wiktionary, "ile" (Yoruba section): ilé house; ilé ọba listed as palace. Wiktionary, "ọba": ọba king. Wiktionary, "fila": fìlà cap.; Wiktionary, "baba" (Yoruba section): bàbá and baba both mean father; baálé (head of the household) is derived from baba + ilé.; The tone of the lengthened vowel in COERLL's spellings varies from phrase to phrase and is not explained there, so this lesson shows no tone-change step. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra things (ìwé, aṣọ, bàtà, àpò, owó, ajá, ológbò), the possessives wa, yín and wọn in use, and the phrases ọkọ̀ wa and ilé bàbá Adé were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `ile-oba`: **ilé ọba** (the king's house (a palace))
  - Parts: ilé + ọba
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists ilé ọba as palace. Confirm that it is also read as "the king's house" in everyday use.
- `fila-akande`: **fìlà Àkàndé** (Àkàndé's cap)
  - Parts: fìlà + Àkàndé
  - Doubt: Taken from Wikipedia, which cites Bamgboṣe and Rowlands. The tones on Àkàndé come from that page only. Confirm.
- `ile-baba`: **ilé bàbá** (father's house)
  - Parts: ilé + bàbá
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives both bàbá and baba for father; COERLL uses bàbá. Confirm which form fits this phrase. The common mistake links bàbá ilé to baálé (baba + ilé), which rests on Wiktionary alone. Confirm.
- `ile-baba-mi`: **ilé bàbá mi** (my father's house)
  - Parts: ilé + bàbá + mi
  - Doubt: Not written out in a source. COERLL writes bàbáà mi, with a lengthened vowel, in Orúkọ bàbáà mi. Confirm the spelling learners should use.
- `oko-wa`: **ọkọ̀ wa** (our vehicle)
  - Parts: ọkọ̀ + wa
  - Doubt: Built on the COERLL possessive table. Confirm the tones.
- `ile-baba-adé`: **ilé bàbá Adé** (Adé's father's house)
  - Parts: ilé + bàbá + Adé
  - Doubt: A chain built on ilé bàbá mi. Confirm that a name can end the chain in this way.

### Words for people: olù- and oní- (`i-3`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "olukọ" (Yoruba section): olùkọ́ teacher, from olù- (a non-productive agent prefix) + kọ́ (to teach), literally "the one who teaches".; Wiktionary, "olufẹ" (Yoruba section): olùfẹ́ lover, loved one, from olù- + fẹ́ (to love).; Wiktionary, "oni-" (Yoruba section): oní- is added to nouns for ownership or association (ilé → onílé, landlord); usage table: before a, e, ẹ, o, ọ, u the prefix changes to alá-, elé-, ẹlẹ́-, oló-, ọlọ́-, onú-, and before i it stays oní-.; Wiktionary, "onile" (onílé, landlord, from oní- + ilé), "ẹlẹja" (ẹlẹ́ja, fish seller, from oní- + ẹja), "alakara" (alákàrà, akara seller, from oní- + àkàrà).; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 9 vocabulary, page 212: olùkọ́ (teacher), oníṣòwò (trader), onídìrí (hair braider), ẹlẹ́ran (meat seller), aláró (tie-dyer). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; The step-by-step vowel changes below follow the Wiktionary table; the table gives the results, not the stages. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra olù- words (olùgbọ́, olùkàwé, olùṣọ́, olùrànlọ́wọ́, olùdarí) and oní- words (oníṣòwò kept from COERLL; olówó, alágbára, ẹlẹ́ran, aláró) and their breakdowns were added for this lesson. Only oníṣòwò, ẹlẹ́ran and aláró appear in a consulted source; the rest were not checked. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `oluko`: **olùkọ́** (teacher)
  - Parts: olù + kọ́
  - Doubt: Wiktionary and COERLL agree on olùkọ́. Wiktionary calls olù- non-productive. Confirm the tones.
- `olufe`: **olùfẹ́** (lover, loved one)
  - Parts: olù + fẹ́
  - Doubt: From Wiktionary only. Confirm the gloss, since fẹ́ also means "want".
- `onile`: **onílé** (landlord)
  - Parts: oní + ilé
  - Steps: oníilé > onílé
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives oní- + ilé = onílé. Describing it as two i sounds merging is an interpretation of that result.
- `eleja`: **ẹlẹ́ja** (fish seller)
  - Parts: oní + ẹja
  - Steps: oníẹja > ẹlẹ́ja
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives oní- + ẹja = ẹlẹ́ja and the ẹlẹ́- row of its table. The single step covers several changes at once (o to ẹ, n to l, i to ẹ, the noun's ẹ merging).
- `alakara`: **alákàrà** (akara (bean fritter) seller)
  - Parts: oní + àkàrà
  - Steps: oníàkàrà > alákàrà
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives oní- + àkàrà = alákàrà, and also lists oníkàrà. The low tone of the noun's first à is lost in the merge. Confirm.
- `olugbo`: **olùgbọ́** (listener)
  - Parts: olù + gbọ́
  - Doubt: Built on olùkọ́ with gbọ́ from Getting Started 1. Confirm the tones.
- `olowo`: **olówó** (rich person)
  - Parts: oní + owó
  - Steps: oníowó > olówó
  - Doubt: Built from the oní- table row for o. Confirm olówó as the usual word and its tones.

### This, that, and plurals (`i-4`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "yii" (Yoruba section): yìí, this, placed after the noun (Ọmọ yìí ni tèmi, this child is mine); a short form of èyí.; Wiktionary, "yẹn" (Yoruba section): yẹn, that, placed after the noun (Mo fẹ́ ka ìwé yẹn, I want to read that book); a short form of ìyẹn. The low-tone yẹ̀n is an Ìjẹ̀bú and Ìkálẹ̀ form.; Wiktionary, "wọnyi" and "wọnyẹn" (Yoruba sections): wọ̀nyí, these; wọ̀nyẹn, those.; Wiktionary, "awọn" (Yoruba section): àwọn, a particle that comes before a noun to mark it as plural; àwọ̀n (low, low) is a different word, net.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 2, Lesson 2 "The Plural marker àwọn", page 55: àwọn goes before the noun (Àwọn ilé, houses; Àwọn ọmọdé, children), is sometimes left out when the plural is clear, and can mark respect for one person. The textbook's exercise headings use àwọn ... wọ̀nyí, as in àwọn ìbéèrè wọ̀nyí (these questions). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra nouns (ọkùnrin, obìnrin), describing words (dára, tuntun, ńlá, kékeré, gíga) and the phrases ilé ńlá yẹn and Ìwé wo were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `omo-yii`: **ọmọ yìí** (this child)
  - Parts: ọmọ + yìí
  - Doubt: Wiktionary spells yìí but describes the sound as a high tone with a downstep (a slight drop in pitch). Confirm how to teach the pitch.
- `omo-yen`: **ọmọ yẹn** (that child)
  - Parts: ọmọ + yẹn
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives yẹn with no mark, also with a downstep. Not written out with ọmọ in a source. Confirm.
- `awon-omo`: **àwọn ọmọ** (children)
  - Parts: àwọn + ọmọ
  - Doubt: COERLL shows the same pattern with àwọn ọmọdé and àwọn ilé. Confirm the tones on àwọn (low, mid).
- `awon-omo-wonyi`: **àwọn ọmọ wọ̀nyí** (these children)
  - Parts: àwọn + ọmọ + wọ̀nyí
  - Doubt: Follows COERLL's àwọn ... wọ̀nyí pattern. Speakers may also say àwọn ọmọ yìí; no consulted source settles this. Confirm.
- `ile-nla-yen`: **ilé ńlá yẹn** (that big house)
  - Parts: ilé + ńlá + yẹn
  - Doubt: Built on ọmọ yẹn and the usual noun + adjective order. Confirm the tones on ńlá and that the adjective comes before yẹn.
- `iwe-wo`: **Ìwé wo** (Which book?)
  - Parts: Ìwé + wo
  - Doubt: Built on Ìlú wo in Getting Started 3. Confirm the tones.

## Advanced

### When two vowels meet (`a-1`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 10, Lesson 2 "Vowel Assimilation, Vowel Lengthening, and Vowel Deletion", page 243: kú + ilé = kúulé, kú + ìrọ̀lẹ́ = kúùrọ̀lẹ́, ojú + irin = ojúurin; assimilation can go either way (kú + alẹ́ = káalẹ́). Page 245: contraction drops one of two vowels that meet across a word boundary: rí ẹja = rẹ́ja (to see fish), ta iṣu = taṣu (to sell yams), ra ẹran = rẹran (to buy meat), ti ọ̀rẹ́ = tọ̀rẹ́. COERLL says assimilation and contraction are two different processes. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", section "Assimilation and elision" (citing Bamgboṣe 1965 and Ward 1952): ra ẹja becomes rẹja (buy fish); elision is absent only in slow, unnatural speech; the spelling follows speech and writes the contracted form as one word; some writers mark a dropped vowel with an apostrophe (n'ílé).; Wiktionary, "ta", "ra", "ẹran", "iṣu" (Yoruba sections): tà, to sell, said ta before a direct object; rà, to buy; ẹran, meat; iṣu, yam.; The practice sentences Mo rẹran, N kò rẹran and Ó taṣu are built from the contractions above and were not found written out in a source.; No source consulted states a general rule for which of the two vowels drops; this lesson teaches each pair as the sources give it. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra nouns (ẹyin, ọbẹ̀, ata) and the contractions rẹyin and tẹyin were added for this lesson by applying the sources' pattern. Not found in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `reran`: **rẹran** (to buy meat)
  - Parts: ra + ẹran
  - Steps: raẹran > rẹran
  - Doubt: COERLL page 245 gives ra ẹran = rẹran. Confirm the mid tones on rẹran.
- `reja-buy`: **rẹja** (to buy fish)
  - Parts: ra + ẹja
  - Steps: raẹja > rẹja
  - Doubt: From Wikipedia only (ra ẹja becomes rẹja). Not found in COERLL. Confirm the tones.
- `reja-see`: **rẹ́ja** (to see fish)
  - Parts: rí + ẹja
  - Steps: ríẹja > rẹ́ja
  - Doubt: COERLL page 245 gives rí ẹja = rẹ́ja. Reading the high mark on ẹ as the tone left behind by rí is an interpretation of that result. Confirm.
- `tasu`: **taṣu** (to sell yams)
  - Parts: ta + iṣu
  - Steps: taiṣu > taṣu
  - Doubt: COERLL page 245 gives ta iṣu = taṣu. Wiktionary lists the verb as tà (low), said ta before an object. Confirm the tones on taṣu.
- `kuule`: **kúulé** (welcome (into the house))
  - Parts: kú + ilé
  - Steps: kúilé > kúulé
  - Doubt: COERLL page 243 gives kú + ilé = kúulé, a greeting to welcome someone into the house. Confirm that the new u is mid tone.
- `reyin`: **rẹyin** (to buy eggs)
  - Parts: ra + ẹyin
  - Steps: raẹyin > rẹyin
  - Doubt: Built on ra ẹran = rẹran. Not found written out in a source. Confirm the tones.
- `teyin`: **tẹyin** (to sell eggs)
  - Parts: ta + ẹyin
  - Steps: taẹyin > tẹyin
  - Doubt: Built on ra ẹran = rẹran, with ta from taṣu. Not found written out in a source. Confirm the tones.

### Verbal nouns: lílọ, jíjẹ, kíkà (`a-2`)
Sources consulted: Inkelas and Zoll, Reduplication: Doubling in Morphology (Cambridge University Press), Chapter 1 excerpt, pages 2 to 3: Yoruba gerunds are made by adding a high-tone í after a copy of the verb's first consonant; data from Kawu 1998: gbé → gbígbé (take, taking), jẹ → jíjẹ (eat, eating), wọ̀ → wíwọ̀ (enter, entering), bú → bíbú (insult, insulting). The book notes that some linguists call this reduplication and others call it copying.; Wiktionary, "jijẹ" (jíjẹ, eating, partial reduplication of jẹ), "ka" (kà, to read or count; derived term kíkà, reading), "mu" (mu, to drink; derived term mímu, drinking), "wa" (wà, to be; derived term wíwà, existence), "gbe" (gbé, to carry; example Gbígbé ni mo gbé e), "lọ" (lọ, to go, mid tone; lọ̀, to grind; lílọ̀, ground).; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin): ìwé kíkà (studies), Chapter 9 vocabulary, page 213; ilé-ìwé lílọ (going to school), Chapter 9, page 221; nǹkan mímu (drinks), Chapter 7, page 170; aṣọ wíwọ̀ (wearing clothes), Chapter 11 title, page 263. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; lílọ is taken from COERLL's ilé-ìwé lílọ; Wiktionary has no entry for it. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra verbal nouns rírìn, sísùn, wíwá, rírí, ṣíṣe, jíjó and kíkọ, and the phrase orin kíkọ, were built for this lesson from the same recipe. Not found in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `lilo`: **lílọ** (going)
  - Parts: lí + lọ
  - Doubt: Found in COERLL's ilé-ìwé lílọ (page 221). No dictionary entry was found. Do not confuse with lílọ̀ (ground), from lọ̀, to grind.
- `jije`: **jíjẹ** (eating)
  - Parts: jí + jẹ
  - Doubt: Wiktionary and Kawu (via Inkelas and Zoll) agree on jíjẹ. Wiktionary also lists jíjẹ as an Ifẹ̀ word for food. Confirm the tones.
- `kika`: **kíkà** (reading, counting)
  - Parts: kí + kà
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists kíkà under kà, and COERLL uses ìwé kíkà. Confirm the low tone on kà is kept.
- `gbigbe`: **gbígbé** (carrying, taking)
  - Parts: gbí + gbé
  - Doubt: From Kawu's data in Inkelas and Zoll, and Wiktionary's example Gbígbé ni mo gbé e. Confirm the gloss.
- `mimu`: **mímu** (drinking)
  - Parts: mí + mu
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists mímu under mu, and COERLL uses nǹkan mímu for drinks. Do not confuse mu (drink) with mú (take).
- `iwe-kika`: **ìwé kíkà** (reading, studies)
  - Parts: ìwé + kíkà
  - Doubt: COERLL glosses ìwé kíkà as "studies" (page 213). Confirm the tones on ìwé.
- `risin`: **rírìn** (walking)
  - Parts: rí + rìn
  - Doubt: Built on the same recipe as jíjẹ. Not found written out in a consulted source. Confirm the tones.
- `sisun`: **sísùn** (sleeping)
  - Parts: sí + sùn
  - Doubt: Built on the same recipe as jíjẹ. Not found written out in a consulted source. Confirm the tones.
- `orin-kiko`: **orin kíkọ** (singing)
  - Parts: orin + kíkọ
  - Doubt: Built on ìwé kíkà. Confirm the phrase, and that kíkọ here comes from the verb kọ (sing a song, write).

### Serial verbs (`a-3`)
Sources consulted: Wikipedia, "Serial verb construction", section "Examples with intervening elements between verbs" (citing Tallerman): ó mú ìwé wá, he brought the book, literally "he took book came"; the subject applies to both verbs.; Ariyo, The definition of head and the syntactic structure of verbs in the composition of Yorùbá serial verb constructions (PhD dissertation, University of Victoria, 2025): page 47, Olú mú ọ̀bẹ gé ẹran (Olú took the knife to cut meat) and Olú ti mú ọ̀bẹ gé ẹran, with ti allowed only before the first verb; pages 52 to 53, Olú mú ìwé wá ilé (Olú brought a book home), Olú kò mú ìwé wá ilé (Olú did not bring a book home), and *Olú mú ìwé kò wá ilé marked ungrammatical, because the negative marker always comes before the first verb.; Wiktionary, "mu" (mú, to take; derived term mú wá, to bring), "gbe" (gbé, to carry or take; example Dáńfó yẹn ń gbé èèyàn lọ sí Muṣin, that danfo bus is taking people to Mushin), "wa" (wá, to come), "lọ" (lọ, to go), "ọbẹ" (ọ̀bẹ, knife; ọbẹ̀, stew).; Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", Grammar section: serial verb constructions are common.; Ó gbé ìwé lọ is built on the gbé ... lọ pattern in Wiktionary's example and was not found written out. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra things (omi, oúnjẹ, aṣọ, búrẹ́dì), the places ọjà and ilé-ìwé, and the sentences Mo mú omi wá ilé, Ó gbé aṣọ lọ and Olú mú ọ̀bẹ gé búrẹ́dì were added for this lesson on the sources' patterns. Not found in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `o-mu-iwe-wa`: **Ó mú ìwé wá** (He brought the book)
  - Parts: Ó + mú + ìwé + wá
  - Doubt: Wikipedia gives ó mú ìwé wá, citing Tallerman. Wiktionary lists mú wá as "to bring". Confirm the tones.
- `olu-mu-iwe-wa-ile`: **Olú mú ìwé wá ilé** (Olú brought a book home)
  - Parts: Olú + mú + ìwé + wá + ilé
  - Doubt: Taken from Ariyo (2025), page 52, which glosses mú as "bring" and wá as "come". Only one source. Confirm the tones.
- `o-gbe-iwe-lo`: **Ó gbé ìwé lọ** (He took the book away)
  - Parts: Ó + gbé + ìwé + lọ
  - Doubt: Built on Wiktionary's gbé èèyàn lọ (taking people away). This exact sentence was not found in a source. Confirm the gloss "took away".
- `olu-mu-obe-ge-eran`: **Olú mú ọ̀bẹ gé ẹran** (Olú took the knife to cut meat)
  - Parts: Olú + mú + ọ̀bẹ + gé + ẹran
  - Doubt: Taken from Ariyo (2025), page 47, which glosses it "Olú took the knife to cut meat". Each verb here has its own object. Confirm the tones on ọ̀bẹ (knife), not ọbẹ̀ (stew).
- `mo-mu-omi-wa-ile`: **Mo mú omi wá ilé** (I brought water home)
  - Parts: Mo + mú + omi + wá + ilé
  - Doubt: Built on Ariyo's Olú mú ìwé wá ilé. Confirm the tones.

### Relative clauses with tí (`a-4`)
Sources consulted: Lawal, "Yoruba relativisation and the continuous segment principle", Studies in African Linguistics 18(1), 1987, pages 68 to 72: tí follows the noun; when the noun is the subject of the clause, a pronoun (ó) stays in the clause, as in ọkùnrin tí ó ra aṣọ (the man who bought the cloth); when the noun is the object, nothing fills its place, as in aṣọ tí ọkùnrin náà rà (the cloth that the man bought). The online PDF text has lost most tone marks; tones here follow Wiktionary.; Wiktionary, "ti" (Yoruba section): tí, relative pronoun "which, who, that", example Adìyẹ tí mo rà (the chicken that I bought).; Wiktionary, "gbe" (Yoruba section): example Ilé tí Bọ́lá ń gbé ò tóbi tó (the house Bọ́lá lives in isn't big enough). Wiktionary, "ra" and "ka": rà, to buy; kà is said ka before a direct object.; Wiktionary, "adiyẹ" (adìyẹ, chicken), "ọkunrin" (ọkùnrin, man), "aṣọ" (aṣọ, cloth).; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 5, page 115: oúnjẹ tí ó dára (food that is good); Chapter 10, page 241: aṣọ tí ó mọ́ (clothes that are clean) and ẹni tó ní ìlera (a person who has good health), where tí ó and tó both appear; COERLL does not say tó comes from tí ó. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; ọmọ tí ó lọ follows Lawal's subject pattern and was not found written out. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra nouns (ìwé, oúnjẹ, obìnrin) and the phrases ìwé tí mo kà, oúnjẹ tí mo jẹ and obìnrin tí ó wá were built for this lesson on the sources' patterns. Not found in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `omo-ti-o-lo`: **ọmọ tí ó lọ** (the child who went)
  - Parts: ọmọ + tí + ó + lọ
  - Doubt: Follows Lawal's subject pattern; not written out in a source. Confirm the tones, and that ó is the usual pronoun here.
- `okunrin-ti-o-ra-aso`: **ọkùnrin tí ó ra aṣọ** (the man who bought the cloth)
  - Parts: ọkùnrin + tí + ó + ra + aṣọ
  - Doubt: From Lawal (1987), example 1, whose text lost its tone marks. Tones follow Wiktionary. Confirm that ra is mid before aṣọ.
- `adiye-ti-mo-ra`: **adìyẹ tí mo rà** (the chicken that I bought)
  - Parts: adìyẹ + tí + mo + rà
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's example under tí, word for word. Wiktionary also lists adìẹ as another spelling. Confirm the tones.
- `ile-ti-bola-n-gbe`: **ilé tí Bọ́lá ń gbé** (the house that Bọ́lá lives in)
  - Parts: ilé + tí + Bọ́lá + ń + gbé
  - Doubt: From Wiktionary's example under gbé (to live). Here the house is the place, and no word like "in" is needed. Confirm.
- `iwe-ti-mo-ka`: **ìwé tí mo kà** (the book that I read)
  - Parts: ìwé + tí + mo + kà
  - Doubt: Built on adìyẹ tí mo rà. Confirm the tones, and that kà is low at the end.
- `ounje-ti-mo-je`: **oúnjẹ tí mo jẹ** (the food that I ate)
  - Parts: oúnjẹ + tí + mo + jẹ
  - Doubt: Built on adìyẹ tí mo rà. Confirm the tones.
- `obinrin-ti-o-wa`: **obìnrin tí ó wá** (the woman who came)
  - Parts: obìnrin + tí + ó + wá
  - Doubt: Built on Lawal's subject pattern, like ọmọ tí ó lọ. Confirm the tones.

## Mastery

### Words that join into one (`m-1`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "nile" (Yoruba section): nílé, at home, a contraction of ní ilé; other forms lúlé (Èkìtì), núlí (Oǹdó, Ìkálẹ̀), ńlé.; Wiktionary, "lọla" (lọ́la, tomorrow, contraction of ní ọ̀la), "lonii" (lónìí, today, contraction of ní òní, pronounced lóní with a downstep on the second syllable; example Kí l'ẹ jẹun lónìí?), "lanaa" (lánàá, yesterday, contraction of ní àná), "ọla" (ọ̀la, tomorrow; ọlà, wealth), "oni" (òní, today).; Wikipedia, "Yoruba language", section "Assimilation and elision": the spelling follows speech, so contracted words are written without a word break; some writers mark a dropped vowel with an apostrophe, as in n'ílé.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 10, page 241: the same passage writes aṣọ tí ó mọ́ (clothes that are clean) and ẹni tó ní ìlera (a person who has good health), so tí ó and tó both appear. COERLL does not explain tó. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; The practice sentences Mo máa lọ lọ́la, Mo máa lọ lónìí and Ọmọ tó lọ were not found written out. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The extra fused forms lọ́jà, lóko, láàárọ̀, lálẹ́ and lọ́sàn-án, and the sentences using wà, were added for this lesson on the pattern of lọ́la. Only lánàá appears in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `nile`: **nílé** (at home)
  - Parts: ní + ilé
  - Steps: níilé > nílé
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives nílé as a contraction of ní ilé. Describing it as two i sounds merging is an interpretation of that result.
- `lola`: **lọ́la** (tomorrow)
  - Parts: ní + ọ̀la
  - Steps: níọ̀la > lọ́la
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives lọ́la from ní ọ̀la, with a downstep on la. The single step covers several changes (n to l, í dropped, ọ̀ taking a high tone). Confirm.
- `lonii`: **lónìí** (today)
  - Parts: ní + òní
  - Steps: níòní > lónìí
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives lónìí from ní òní and says it sounds like lóní with a downstep. Reading the ìí spelling as a way of writing that drop is an interpretation. The single step covers several changes. Lagos lénìí and Èkìtì lóòní are also listed. Confirm.
- `to`: **tó** (who, that (with he or she))
  - Parts: tí + ó
  - Steps: tíó > tó
  - Doubt: COERLL writes both tí ó and tó on page 241 but does not state that tó comes from tí ó. That link is an interpretation. Confirm.
- `loja`: **lọ́jà** (at the market)
  - Parts: ní + ọjà
  - Steps: níọjà > lọ́jà
  - Doubt: Built on lọ́la. Not written out in a consulted source. Confirm the tones.
- `lanaa`: **lánàá** (yesterday)
  - Parts: ní + àná
  - Steps: níàná > lánàá
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives lánàá from ní àná. The single step covers several changes. Confirm the tones.

### Focus with ni (`m-2`)
Sources consulted: Jones, "Focus in Yorùbá: a semantic/pragmatic account", ZAS Papers in Linguistics 46 (2006), pages 143 to 144: the focused part goes first and is followed by ni; when a subject is focused, the pronoun ó is obligatory (citing Carstens 1985): Adé ni ó ra ìwé; a focused object leaves a gap: Ìwé ni Adé rà. Page 148: Akin ni ó lo (printed without the dot under o), answering Ta ni ó lo? (who went?).; Ariyo, The definition of head and the syntactic structure of verbs in the composition of Yorùbá serial verb constructions (PhD dissertation, University of Victoria, 2025), page 51: Olú kọ́ ni ó gbé igi (it was not Olú who carried a stick); page 61: Olú ni ó lọ ata se ọbẹ̀ (it was Olú who ground pepper to cook stew).; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 5, page 116: ni is negated as kọ́ ni. Chapter 4, page 90: Ìwé mẹ́ta ni ó wà lóríi tábìlì (there are three books on the table). Chapter 10, page 241: Ìmọ́tótó ló lè ṣẹ́gun àrùn gbogbo (cleanliness can conquer every disease), where the form ló is attested; reading ló as ni ó is the author's inference, not something COERLL states. The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Wiktionary, "rọ" (Yoruba section), example Alágbẹ̀dẹ ló bá mi rọ àdá yẹn (it was a blacksmith that forged that cutlass for me).; Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The questions Ta ni ó lọ and Kí ni Adé rà, and the answers Bùkọ́lá ni ó ra aṣọ and Ìwé ni Adé rà in question and answer form, were added for this lesson on Jones's patterns. Ta ni ó lo appears in Jones (2006); the others were not found in a consulted source. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `ade-ni-o-ra-iwe`: **Adé ni ó ra ìwé** (It was Adé who bought the book)
  - Parts: Adé + ni + ó + ra + ìwé
  - Doubt: Jones (2006), example 3, word for word. Jones glosses it "[Ade] bought a/the book". Confirm the tones. The lesson also reads ló as ni ó (see the rule on ló); that reading is the author's inference, and COERLL does not state it.
- `iwe-ni-ade-ra`: **Ìwé ni Adé rà** (It was a book that Adé bought)
  - Parts: Ìwé + ni + Adé + rà
  - Doubt: Jones (2006), example 4, word for word. Confirm the low tone on rà.
- `akin-ni-o-lo`: **Akin ni ó lọ** (It was Akin who went)
  - Parts: Akin + ni + ó + lọ
  - Doubt: Jones (2006), example 16, prints lo without the dot under o; lọ (go) follows Wiktionary. Confirm.
- `olu-ko-ni-o-gbe-igi`: **Olú kọ́ ni ó gbé igi** (It was not Olú who carried the stick)
  - Parts: Olú + kọ́ + ni + ó + gbé + igi
  - Doubt: Ariyo (2025), example 42, word for word. COERLL gives kọ́ ni as the negative of ni. Confirm the tones.
- `ta-ni-o-lo`: **Ta ni ó lọ** (Who went?)
  - Parts: Ta ni + ó + lọ
  - Doubt: Jones (2006) gives Ta ni ó lo? as the question for Akin ni ó lọ. Confirm the tones.
- `ki-ni-ade-ra`: **Kí ni Adé rà** (What did Adé buy?)
  - Parts: Kí ni + Adé + rà
  - Doubt: Built on Ìwé ni Adé rà with kí ni from Getting Started 2. Confirm the tones.

### Complex sentences: if and because (`m-3`)
Sources consulted: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 4 vocabulary, page 89: tí..bá, if; nítorí pé, because. Chapter 12, page 290, a song: Bí o bá kàwé rẹ, bàtà rẹ á dún ko ko kà, and Bí o ò bá kàwé rẹ ... Chapter 11, page 279: Mà á lọ sí ilé nígbà tí mo bá parí ìwé tí mò ń kà (I will go home when I finish the book I'm reading). Chapter 8, page 202: Ṣebí dókítà kò níí fún mi ní abẹ́rẹ́ nínú ojú (the doctor will not give me an injection in the eye, will he?). The PDF uses a legacy font encoding for tone marks.; Wiktionary, "bi" (Yoruba section): bí, conditional "if"; another bí means "when, as soon as" (Bí a ti jáde ni wọ́n dé, as soon as we left, they arrived).; Wiktionary, "rọ" (rọ̀, to fall, of rain; example Òjò máa rọ̀, it will rain) and "ojo" (òjò, rain). Wiktionary, "rọ", second sense: Tẹ́ ẹ bá gbàgbé ..., òdòdó náà á rọ (if you forget ..., the flower will wilt), another if-clause with bá.; Wiktionary, "nitori" (nítorí, on account of, because of) and "pe" (pé, that).; Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (citing Adebayo 2021 and others): the negative future is written kò níí; ò is a short form of kò after pronouns. Wiktionary examples elsewhere write kò ní and kò ní í.; The sentences Bí òjò bá rọ̀, A ò níí lọ, and Nítorí pé òjò rọ̀ are built from these sources and were not found written out. Not cross-checked against a print grammar.; The joining words ṣùgbọ́n, tàbí and nígbà tí, the weather words (oòrùn, afẹ́fẹ́, òtútù, ooru) and the sentence Mo fẹ́ lọ, ṣùgbọ́n òjò ń rọ̀ were added for this lesson. Not checked against a consulted source here. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `bi-ojo-ba-ro`: **Bí òjò bá rọ̀** (If it rains)
  - Parts: Bí + òjò + bá + rọ̀
  - Doubt: Built from Wiktionary's Òjò máa rọ̀ and COERLL's bí ... bá pattern; not written out in a source. Confirm the low tone on rọ̀.
- `a-o-nii-lo`: **A ò níí lọ** (We will not go)
  - Parts: A + kò + níí + lọ
  - Steps: A kò níí lọ > A ò níí lọ
  - Doubt: COERLL writes kò níí (page 202). Wiktionary also writes kò ní and kò ní í. A ò níí lọ itself was not found. Confirm the spelling.
- `bi-o-ba-kawe-re`: **Bí o bá kàwé rẹ** (If you read your books)
  - Parts: Bí + o + bá + kàwé + rẹ
  - Doubt: From the COERLL song on page 290. COERLL gives no English line for it; "if you read your books" is our gloss. kàwé is read as ka + ìwé contracted, as in Advanced 1, where kà is said ka before an object; the song prints it with a low tone on kà. Confirm.
- `nitori-pe-ojo-ro`: **Nítorí pé òjò rọ̀** (Because it rained)
  - Parts: Nítorí + pé + òjò + rọ̀
  - Doubt: COERLL lists nítorí pé as "because". The bare verb rọ̀ is read as past, as COERLL reads Mo lọ (I went). Not written out in a source. Confirm.
- `mo-fe-lo-sugbon`: **Mo fẹ́ lọ, ṣùgbọ́n òjò ń rọ̀** (I want to go, but it is raining)
  - Parts: Mo + fẹ́ + lọ, + ṣùgbọ́n + òjò + ń + rọ̀
  - Doubt: Built from fẹ́ and ń rọ̀ with ṣùgbọ́n as a common word for but. Not checked against a consulted source. Confirm the tones.

### Proverbs (òwe) (`m-4`)
Sources consulted: Afolayan, "Ọmọ Tí A Kò Kọ́: Globalization and Cultural Education among New Generation Nigerian Yorùbá", Yoruba Studies Review 3(1), 2021, abstract and body: the proverb Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà, translated "the child that is not taught will eventually sell the house that is built"; the essay discusses the play on kọ́ (teach) and kọ́ (build).; Wiktionary, "oni" (Yoruba section), quoting Awoyale, Global Yoruba Lexical Database v. 1.0 (Linguistic Data Consortium, 2008): Òní l'a rí, ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la, translated "It is [only] today that we see, [only] the Most High sees tomorrow", a proverb on divine supremacy.; Wiktionary, "kọ" (kọ́, to build; kọ́, to teach or learn; kọ̀, to refuse; kọ, to write), "ta" (tà, to sell, said ta before a direct object), "oke" (òkè, hill, top, high), "ọla" (ọ̀la, tomorrow), "ri" (rí, to see). ọba (king) is as taught in Intermediate 2.; COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi (University of Texas at Austin), Chapter 11 vocabulary, page 262: pa òwe, to say proverbially.; Each proverb was found in one source only. Neither was checked with a speaker or a print collection of proverbs, so every tone and word is a high doubt.; The word table is made from the proverbs here and on the Proverbs page. The three extra proverbs are the Wiktionary-sourced entries on that page (Ààrò kì í gbóná kalẹ́, Bí ewé bá pẹ́ lára ọṣẹ, á dọṣẹ, and Bí iná bá jó lóko, màjàlà á fò wá sílé). Not checked with a speaker. Needs a native speaker's check before verifying.

- `omo-ti-a-ko-ko`: **Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́** (The child who is not taught)
  - Parts: Ọmọ + tí + a + kò + kọ́
  - Doubt: Proverb wording from Afolayan (2021) only. Very high doubt: confirm every tone, and the reading of a as people in general.
- `ile-ti-a-ko`: **ilé tí a kọ́** (the house that is built)
  - Parts: ilé + tí + a + kọ́
  - Doubt: Part of the Afolayan (2021) proverb. Wiktionary gives kọ́ for both build and teach. Very high doubt: confirm with a speaker.
- `owe-omo`: **Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà** (The child who is not taught will sell the house that is built)
  - Parts: Ọmọ + tí + a + kò + kọ́ + ni + yóò + gbé + ilé + tí + a + kọ́ + tà
  - Doubt: Word for word from Afolayan (2021). Very high doubt. Mastery 2 shows ó after ni with a plain verb, but this proverb has ni yóò with no ó after ni; no consulted source explains this. It also sits outside the Intermediate 1 pattern of yóò with a long pronoun. Both are open doubts. Confirm.
- `oni-la-ri`: **Òní l'a rí** (It is only today that we see)
  - Parts: Òní + l' + a + rí
  - Doubt: From the proverb quoted under Wiktionary's "oni", citing the Global Yoruba Lexical Database. Very high doubt: confirm the wording and the apostrophe spelling. Reading l' as ni is the author's inference; no source states it.
- `oba-oke-lo-rola`: **ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la** (only the Most High sees tomorrow)
  - Parts: ọba + òkè + l' + ó + rọ́la
  - Doubt: Second half of the same Wiktionary proverb. ọba òkè is translated "the Most High" there. Reading rọ́la as rí + ọ̀la is our interpretation, and so is reading l'ó as ni ó (the ló doubt from Mastery 2): no source states either. Very high doubt: confirm.
- `owe-ina`: **Bí iná bá jó lóko, màjàlà á fò wá sílé** (When a fire burns in the field, the soot flies home)
  - Parts: Bí + iná + bá + jó + lóko, + màjàlà + á + fò + wá + sílé
  - Doubt: From the Proverbs page, which takes it from Wiktionary. Uses bí ... bá from Mastery 3, fused lóko from Mastery 1 and fò wá as a two-verb chain from Advanced 3. Reading á as the short form of máa is the author's guess. Very high doubt: confirm the tones.

## Vocab

### Getting Started

#### Greetings and courtesies (`gs-greetings`)
Sources consulted: src/content/lessons/gs-3.ts, this app's greetings lesson (itself sourced from COERLL Yorùbá Yé Mi and Wiktionary): ẹ káàárọ̀, ẹ káalẹ́, ẹ kúuṣẹ́ and the respectful ẹ. Not rechecked against COERLL for this topic.; Wiktionary, "kaasan" (Yoruba section): káàsán, good afternoon, used between noon and 4 pm; ẹ comes first when speaking respectfully.; Wiktionary, "ẹ ṣeun" (Yoruba section): thank you, honorific or to several people; o ṣeun is the plain form.; Wiktionary, "bawo" (Yoruba section): báwo, how; derived term báwo ni?.; University of Georgia, Yoruba unit 8 culture page "Greetings in Yoruba": E kaabo (welcome, to a senior or a group), written without tone marks.; The model's own knowledge for the tone marks of ẹ káàbọ̀, ẹ jọ̀ọ́, ẹ má bínú and ó dàbọ̀, and for all ten example sentences. No tone-marked dictionary entry for those four was found while drafting.

- `gs-greetings-kaaro`: **ẹ káàárọ̀** (good morning, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàárọ̀, oòrùn ti yọ. / Good morning, the sun is up.
  - Doubt: Same form as lesson gs-3. ẹ shows respect; to a friend, káàárọ̀ alone. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm that oòrùn ti yọ (the sun is up) sounds natural.
- `gs-greetings-kaasan`: **ẹ káàsán** (good afternoon, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàsán, ọ̀sán yìí gbóná. / Good afternoon, it is hot this afternoon.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives káàsán, used from noon to about 4 pm. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm gbóná (hot) for the weather, rather than a phrase with oòrùn (sun).
- `gs-greetings-kaale`: **ẹ káalẹ́** (good evening, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káalẹ́, alẹ́ yìí tutù. / Good evening, it is cool this evening.
  - Doubt: Same form as lesson gs-3. Confirm the mid tone on the second a. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm tutù (cool, cold) for the evening air.
- `gs-greetings-kuuse`: **ẹ kúuṣẹ́** (well done (to someone working), phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ kúuṣẹ́, ẹ ṣì ń kọ́lé. / Well done, you are still building.
  - Doubt: Same joined form as lesson gs-3; some write it as ẹ kú iṣẹ́. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm ṣì ń kọ́lé (still building a house) is natural.
- `gs-greetings-kaabo`: **ẹ káàbọ̀** (welcome, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ káàbọ̀ sí ilé wa. / Welcome to our home.
  - Doubt: Tone marks from the model's own knowledge (kú + àbọ̀, joined like káàárọ̀). The one source checked writes E kaabo without tone marks. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-greetings-seun`: **ẹ ṣeun** (thank you, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ ṣeun fún ẹ̀bùn yìí. / Thank you for this gift.
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives ẹ ṣeun (o ṣeun to a friend). The shorter ẹ ṣé is also common. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm fún (for) is the natural way to give the reason.
- `gs-greetings-jowo`: **ẹ jọ̀ọ́** (please, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ jọ̀ọ́, ẹ ràn mí lọ́wọ́. / Please help me.
  - Doubt: Tone marks from the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Some write the fuller ẹ jọ̀wọ́. Sentence drafted by the model.
- `gs-greetings-binu`: **ẹ má bínú** (sorry, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ má bínú, mo pẹ́. / Sorry, I am late.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Literally "do not be angry". Sentence drafted by the model: confirm mo pẹ́ (I am late) is the natural phrasing.
- `gs-greetings-dabo`: **ó dàbọ̀** (goodbye, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ó dàbọ̀, a ó tún rí ara wa. / Goodbye, we will see each other again.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; no source checked. Confirm the tones and whether it is written as two words or as one (ódàbọ̀). Sentence drafted by the model: confirm a ó (we will) versus a óò.
- `gs-greetings-bawo`: **báwo ni** (how are you?, phrase)
  - Sentence: Báwo ni ara rẹ? / How are you?
  - Doubt: Wiktionary gives báwo (how) and báwo ni?. Casual, for friends; to an elder a greeting with ẹ is more polite. Sentence drafted by the model; báwo ni ara rẹ is literally "how is your body".

#### Family (`gs-family`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "aburo" (Yoruba section): àbúrò, younger sibling, with regional forms àbúò and àbú; coordinate term ẹ̀gbọ́n.; Wiktionary, "ẹgbọn" (Yoruba section): ẹ̀gbọ́n, older sibling, extended to an older relative of the same generation.; src/content/audio-manifest.json and this app's grammar lessons: ọmọ and ọkọ are there as words of their own; bàbá and ọ̀rẹ́ appear only inside phrases (ilé bàbá mi, òun ni ọ̀rẹ́ mi). Same spellings. Not rechecked against an outside source.; The model's own knowledge for ìyá, ìyá àgbà, bàbá àgbà, ìyàwó and for all ten example sentences.

- `gs-family-iya`: **ìyá** (mother, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìyá mi fún mi lọ́mú. / My mother breastfed me.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge. Màmá is also common in everyday speech. Sentence drafted by the model: confirm fún ... lọ́mú (breastfeed) is natural.
- `gs-family-baba`: **bàbá** (father, noun)

### Advanced

#### Market and money (`a-market`)

  - Sentence: Ẹni tí ó ń ra ọjà lọ́wọ́ oníṣòwò ni a ń pè ní oníbàárà. / A person who buys goods from a trader is called a customer.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition, so only customer fits; confirm pè ní (call, name).

  - Doubt: Same spelling as the COERLL list quoted in the grammar lesson sources. Sentence drafted by the model: buying to resell defines a trader, not a customer or a rich person; confirm ní owó kékeré (cheaply) and ju bẹ́ẹ̀ lọ (more than that).
- `a-market-iye-owo`: **iye owó** (price, noun)
  - Sentence: Mo béèrè iye owó bàtà náà lọ́wọ́ oníṣòwò kí n tó rà á. / I asked the trader the price of the shoes before I bought them.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally amount of money); speakers often just say owó or ask Èló ni? Sentence drafted by the model: asking before buying rules out change, profit and debt; confirm béèrè ... lọ́wọ́ (ask someone) and rà á.
- `a-market-senji`: **ṣẹ́ǹjì** (change (money given back), noun)
  - Sentence: Mo fún un ní ẹgbẹ̀rún náírà fún ọjà ẹgbẹ̀rin náírà, ó sì dá ṣẹ́ǹjì igba náírà padà fún mi. / I gave him a thousand naira for goods costing eight hundred naira, and he gave me back two hundred naira in change.
  - Doubt: A loanword from English change, from the model's own knowledge; confirm the spelling and tones and whether a native word is preferred. Sentence drafted by the model: the arithmetic singles out change; confirm ẹgbẹ̀rún (1000), ẹgbẹ̀rin (800), igba (200) and dá ... padà (give back).
- `a-market-ere`: **èrè** (profit, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ra ọjà náà ní ọgọ́rùn-ún náírà, ó tà á ní igba náírà, nítorí náà èrè rẹ̀ jẹ́ ọgọ́rùn-ún náírà. / She bought the goods for one hundred naira and sold them for two hundred, so her profit was one hundred naira.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: selling above cost rules out àdánù (loss); the verb jèrè (make a profit) was avoided because it contains èrè. Confirm ọgọ́rùn-ún (100).

  - Sentence: Mo yá ẹgbẹ̀rún náírà lọ́wọ́ rẹ̀ ní oṣù tó kọjá, mo sì ti san gbèsè náà padà. / I borrowed a thousand naira from him last month, and I have paid the debt back.
