### Mastery

#### Big ideas (`m-abstract`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ominira" (Yoruba section): òmìnira, freedom, independence, liberty.; Wiktionary, "alaafia" (Yoruba section): àlàáfíà, physical well-being and calm, used in greetings (Ṣé àlàáfíà ni?).; Wiktionary, "suuru" (Yoruba section): sùúrù, calmly enduring delay or hardship.; Wiktionary, "ireti" (Yoruba section): ìrètí, hope, expectation, from ì- + retí (expect).; Wiktionary, "agbara" (Yoruba section): agbára, physical strength, capacity, authority or power (agbàrà, àgbàrá and others are different words).; Wiktionary returned no page for ogbon, ọgbọ́n, otito or igbagbo. The model's own knowledge for òtítọ́, ọgbọ́n, ìdájọ́ òdodo, ìmọ̀ and ìgbàgbọ́, and for all ten example sentences.

- `m-abstract-otito`: **òtítọ́** (truth, noun)
  - Sentence: Òdìkejì irọ́ ni òtítọ́. / The opposite of a lie is the truth.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones (òótọ́ is a common shorter form). Sentence drafted by the model: the opposite of irọ́ (lie) singles out truth; confirm òdìkejì (opposite).
- `m-abstract-ominira`: **òmìnira** (freedom, noun)
  - Sentence: Òdìkejì oko ẹrú ni òmìnira. / The opposite of slavery is freedom.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary, which also gives independence (of a nation). Sentence drafted by the model: the opposite of slavery singles out freedom, not power or peace; confirm oko ẹrú for slavery.
- `m-abstract-ogbon`: **ọgbọ́n** (wisdom, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìmọ̀ ni mímọ̀ nǹkan, ṣùgbọ́n ọgbọ́n ni mímọ̀ bí a ṣe ń lò ó dáadáa. / Knowledge is knowing things, but wisdom is knowing how to use them well.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; confirm the tones, since ọgbọ̀n (thirty) differs only in tone. Sentence drafted by the model: the contrast with ìmọ̀ (named in the sentence) singles out wisdom; the verbal noun mímọ̀ (knowing, from mọ̀) is the reviewer's correction of the model's untoned mímọ; confirm its tones (mímọ́ means pure) and lò ó (use it).
- `m-abstract-idajo-ododo`: **ìdájọ́ òdodo** (justice, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìdájọ́ òdodo ni kí adájọ́ dá ẹjọ́ láìṣe ojúsàájú, kí ó má sì gba àbẹ̀tẹ́lẹ̀. / Justice is when a judge decides a case without favoritism and takes no bribe.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally righteous judgment); speakers may also say òdodo or ìdájọ́ alone. Confirm the form and tones. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition about judging fairly; confirm ojúsàájú (partiality) and àbẹ̀tẹ́lẹ̀ (bribe).
- `m-abstract-suuru`: **sùúrù** (patience, noun)
  - Sentence: Ẹni tí ó ní sùúrù lè dúró de bọ́ọ̀sì fún wákàtí méjì láìbínú. / Someone with patience can wait two hours for the bus without getting angry.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: waiting calmly singles out patience; confirm dúró de (wait for) and láìbínú (without anger).
- `m-abstract-imo`: **ìmọ̀** (knowledge, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ti ka ìwé púpọ̀, ó sì mọ ìtàn Yorùbá dáadáa, ṣùgbọ́n ìmọ̀ nìkan kọ́ ni ọgbọ́n. / She has read many books and knows Yoruba history well, but knowledge alone is not wisdom.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: reading and knowing history is knowledge, and the contrast with ọgbọ́n (named in the sentence) rules wisdom out; confirm mọ (know, said mọ before an object) and nìkan kọ́ ni (alone is not).
- `m-abstract-ireti`: **ìrètí** (hope, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ti kùnà lẹ́ẹ̀mejì, ṣùgbọ́n ó ṣì ń retí pé òun yóò yege; èyí ni ìrètí rẹ̀. / She has failed twice, but she still expects to pass; that is her hope.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Sentence drafted by the model: still expecting (retí) to pass after failing names hope, which comes from retí; faith (ìgbàgbọ́) is about belief, not expectation. Confirm kùnà (fail), yege (pass) and èyí ni (this is).
- `m-abstract-igbagbo`: **ìgbàgbọ́** (faith, noun)
  - Sentence: Bí wọn kò tilẹ̀ rí Ọlọ́run rí, àwọn olùjọsìn ní ìgbàgbọ́ pé ó wà. / Although they have never seen God, worshippers have faith that he exists.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from gbàgbọ́, believe), not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: believing without seeing singles out faith; confirm olùjọsìn (worshipper) and rí ... rí (have ever seen).
- `m-abstract-alaafia`: **àlàáfíà** (peace, noun)
  - Sentence: Ogun ti parí, àlàáfíà sì ti padà sí ìlú wa. / The war has ended, and peace has returned to our town.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary, which glosses it as well-being and calm; it is also the word in greetings such as Ṣé àlàáfíà ni? Sentence drafted by the model: what returns when war ends is peace; confirm ogun (war, not ogún, twenty or inheritance).
- `m-abstract-agbara`: **agbára** (power, strength, noun)
  - Sentence: Erin ní agbára ju ènìyàn mẹ́wàá lọ. / An elephant has more strength than ten people.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary, which gives both strength and authority; do not confuse with àgbàrá (flood). Sentence drafted by the model: physical strength compared with people singles out this word; confirm ju ... lọ (more than).

#### Character and virtue (`m-virtues`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "iwa" (Yoruba section): ìwà, character, behavior; derived terms include ìwà pẹ̀lẹ́ (no gloss given) and hùwà (to behave).; Wiktionary returned no page for omoluabi or owo (respect). ọ̀wọ̀ was not found there. The model's own knowledge for ọ̀wọ̀, ìrẹ̀lẹ̀, ọmọlúàbí, inú rere, ìgboyà, ìtẹ́lọ́rùn, ìlawọ́ and ìfaradà, for the gloss of ìwà pẹ̀lẹ́, for every cultural note, and for all ten example sentences.

- `m-virtues-iwa`: **ìwà** (character, noun)
  - Sentence: Gbogbo ènìyàn ló ní ìwà tirẹ̀; ó lè dára, ó sì lè burú. / Everyone has their own character; it can be good or it can be bad.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. Cultural note (model's own knowledge): ìwà is central to Yoruba ethics; a person is judged by ìwà more than by wealth or looks, as in the saying Ìwà lẹ̀wà (character is beauty). Sentence drafted by the model: only plain character can be good or bad; the virtues in this topic cannot be bad.
- `m-virtues-iwa-pele`: **ìwà pẹ̀lẹ́** (gentle character, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó ní ìwà pẹ̀lẹ́: kì í yára bínú, kì í sì í pariwo mọ́ ẹnikẹ́ni. / She has a gentle character: she is slow to anger and never shouts at anyone.
  - Doubt: Listed on Wiktionary under ìwà without a gloss; the gloss is the model's. Cultural note (model's own knowledge): ìwà pẹ̀lẹ́ (calm, gentle character) is the ideal held up in Ifá teaching, often linked with the òrìṣà Ọbàtálá. Sentence drafted by the model: being slow to anger singles out gentleness, not humility or courage.
- `m-virtues-owo`: **ọ̀wọ̀** (respect, noun)
  - Sentence: Láti fi ọ̀wọ̀ hàn fún àgbà, ọkùnrin Yorùbá máa ń dọ̀bálẹ̀, obìnrin sì máa ń kúnlẹ̀. / To show respect to elders, Yoruba men prostrate and women kneel.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Cultural note: respect for age is shown in the body (dọ̀bálẹ̀, prostrate; kúnlẹ̀, kneel) and in speech (ẹ, the respectful you). Sentence drafted by the model: greeting elders by bowing down singles out respect.
- `m-virtues-irele`: **ìrẹ̀lẹ̀** (humility, noun)
  - Sentence: Bí ó tilẹ̀ jẹ́ olówó, ó ní ìrẹ̀lẹ̀, kì í fi ọrọ̀ rẹ̀ yangàn. / Although he is rich, he has humility; he does not boast about his wealth.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from rẹ̀ sílẹ̀, lower oneself); confirm the tones. Cultural note: a wealthy or titled person who stays humble is especially praised. Sentence drafted by the model: not boasting despite wealth singles out humility; confirm ọrọ̀ (wealth) and yangàn (boast).
- `m-virtues-omoluabi`: **ọmọlúàbí** (person of good character, noun)
  - Sentence: Ẹni tí ó ń bọ̀wọ̀ fún àgbà, tí ó ń sọ òtítọ́, tí ó sì ń ṣiṣẹ́ kára ni àwọn Yorùbá ń pè ní ọmọlúàbí. / Someone who respects elders, tells the truth and works hard is what the Yoruba call a person of good character.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Cultural note: ọmọlúàbí is the Yoruba ideal of a well-brought-up, honest, respectful person; its literal breakdown is debated. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition of a person, while every other word names a quality.
- `m-virtues-inu-rere`: **inú rere** (kindness, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó fi inú rere tọ́jú ajá tí ó farapa tí ó rí lójú ọ̀nà. / With kindness he cared for an injured dog he found on the road.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally good inside); confirm the form. Cultural note: Yoruba often places feelings and intentions in inú (the inside, belly), so a kind person has a good inside. Sentence drafted by the model: caring for a hurt animal singles out kindness; respect or humility do not fit an animal.
- `m-virtues-igboya`: **ìgboyà** (courage, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó fi ìgboyà wọ inú ilé tí ń jó láti gba ọmọ kékeré kan là. / With courage he went into the burning house to save a small child.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; confirm the tones. Cultural note: courage is praised in oríkì (praise poetry) of hunters and warriors. Sentence drafted by the model: entering a fire singles out courage, not endurance; confirm gba ... là (save).
- `m-virtues-itelorun`: **ìtẹ́lọ́rùn** (contentment, noun)
  - Sentence: Ẹni tí ó ní ìtẹ́lọ́rùn kì í wá ju ohun tí ó ní lọ; ohun tí ó ní ti tó fún un. / A person with contentment does not seek more than he has; what he has is enough for him.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from tẹ́ lọ́rùn, satisfy); confirm the tones. Cultural note: contentment is set against ojúkòkòrò (greed), a vice often warned against in proverbs. Sentence drafted by the model: being satisfied with what one has singles out contentment.
- `m-virtues-ilawo`: **ìlawọ́** (generosity, noun)
  - Sentence: Òdìkejì ìwà ahun ni ìlawọ́. / The opposite of stinginess is generosity.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally opening of the hand); confirm the form and tones, and whether ọ̀làwọ́ (a generous person) is more usual. Cultural note: generosity to guests and the needy is a mark of good ìwà. Sentence drafted by the model: the opposite of ìwà ahun (stingy behavior) singles out generosity; confirm ahun.
- `m-virtues-ifarada`: **ìfaradà** (endurance, noun)
  - Sentence: Ó fi ìfaradà ṣiṣẹ́ àṣekára fún ọdún mẹ́wàá, kò jáwọ́ títí ó fi kọ́ ilé tirẹ̀. / With endurance she worked hard for ten years and did not give up until she built her own house.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from fara da, bear with the body); confirm the tones. Cultural note: bearing hardship without complaint is admired, as in the well-known saying Sùúrù ni baba ìwà (patience is the father of character). Sentence drafted by the model: ten years of hard work without giving up singles out endurance, not courage; confirm jáwọ́ (give up).

#### Law and custom (`m-custom`)
Sources consulted: Wiktionary, "ofin" (Yoruba section): òfin, law, rule, legislation.; Wiktionary, "ogun" (Yoruba section): ogún, heritage, inheritance, legacy; also ogún, twenty, and ogun, war.; Wiktionary, "eewo" gave only eéwo (boil, blister), not taboo; èèwọ̀ was not found. Wiktionary returned no page for àṣà.; The model's own knowledge for àṣà, adájọ́, ilé ẹjọ́, ẹ̀wọ̀n, ìbúra, èèwọ̀, ìgbéyàwó and ìsọmọlórúkọ, for the naming-day custom, and for all ten example sentences.

- `m-custom-ofin`: **òfin** (law, noun)
  - Sentence: Ìjọba ti ṣe òfin tuntun pé kí ẹnikẹ́ni má mu sìgá ní gbangba. / The government has made a new law that no one may smoke in public.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary, which also gives rule. Sentence drafted by the model: a government makes laws, not customs or taboos; confirm mu sìgá (smoke) and ní gbangba (in public).
- `m-custom-asa`: **àṣà** (custom, tradition, noun)
  - Sentence: Kò sí òfin tó pa á láṣẹ, ṣùgbọ́n àṣà Yorùbá ni pé kí ọmọdé kí àgbà ní àárọ̀. / No law commands it, but it is Yoruba custom that a young person greets elders in the morning.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge, not found on Wiktionary; confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: òfin is ruled out in the sentence itself, and a greeting habit is not a taboo (a taboo forbids something); confirm pa ... láṣẹ (command).
- `m-custom-adajo`: **adájọ́** (judge, noun)
  - Sentence: Adájọ́ náà gbọ́ ẹ̀rí àwọn méjèèjì kí ó tó dá ẹjọ́ náà. / The judge heard the evidence of both sides before deciding the case.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (one who decides cases); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: the only person word in the topic, and hearing evidence then deciding is what a judge does; confirm ẹ̀rí (evidence) and dá ẹjọ́ (decide a case).
- `m-custom-ile-ejo`: **ilé ẹjọ́** (court, noun)
  - Sentence: Wọ́n mú olè náà lọ sí ilé ẹjọ́, adájọ́ sì rán an lọ sí ẹ̀wọ̀n. / They took the thief to court, and the judge sent him to prison.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally house of cases); confirm whether ilé-ẹjọ́ is written with a hyphen. Sentence drafted by the model: prison and judge are named in the sentence, so only court fits the place the thief goes first.
- `m-custom-ewon`: **ẹ̀wọ̀n** (prison, noun)
  - Sentence: Wọ́n sọ ọkùnrin náà sí ẹ̀wọ̀n fún ọdún márùn-ún nítorí pé ó jalè. / They put the man in prison for five years because he stole.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (also chain); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: being held for five years singles out prison, not court; confirm sọ ... sí ẹ̀wọ̀n (throw into prison) and jalè (steal).
- `m-custom-ibura`: **ìbúra** (oath, noun)
  - Sentence: Kí ẹlẹ́rìí náà tó sọ̀rọ̀ ní ilé ẹjọ́, ó ṣe ìbúra pé òtítọ́ nìkan ni òun yóò sọ. / Before the witness spoke in court, he took an oath that he would tell only the truth.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (from búra, swear); confirm the form, since the verb búra alone may be more natural. Sentence drafted by the model: promising to tell only the truth singles out oath; court is named in the sentence.
- `m-custom-eewo`: **èèwọ̀** (taboo, noun)
  - Sentence: Ohun tí àṣà ìbílẹ̀ kò gbà kí ẹnikẹ́ni ṣe ni a ń pè ní èèwọ̀. / Something traditional custom does not allow anyone to do is called a taboo.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge; Wiktionary's eewo page has only eéwo (boil), so confirm the tones. Cultural note: èèwọ̀ often differ by town, family or òrìṣà. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition; a law comes from government, not custom.
- `m-custom-igbeyawo`: **ìgbéyàwó** (marriage, wedding, noun)
  - Sentence: Ní ìgbéyàwó ìbílẹ̀, ẹbí ọkọ máa ń kó ẹ̀bùn lọ sí ilé ẹbí ìyàwó. / At a traditional wedding, the husband's family takes gifts to the bride's family's house.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally carrying a wife); confirm the tones. Sentence drafted by the model: husband, bride and gifts single out the wedding, not the naming ceremony; confirm ẹ̀bùn (gift) and ìbílẹ̀ (traditional).
- `m-custom-isomolooruko`: **ìsọmọlórúkọ** (naming ceremony, noun)
  - Sentence: Ní ọjọ́ kẹjọ lẹ́yìn tí a bí ọmọ, ẹbí máa ń péjọ fún ìsọmọlórúkọ. / On the eighth day after a child is born, the family gathers for the naming ceremony.
  - Doubt: From the model's own knowledge (literally giving a child a name); confirm the spelling. Cultural note: the eighth day is common today; older sources give the seventh day for girls, the ninth for boys and the eighth for twins. Sentence drafted by the model: a newborn singles out the naming ceremony.
- `m-custom-ogun`: **ogún** (inheritance, noun)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí bàbá wọn kú, àwọn ọmọ rẹ̀ pín ilé àti ilẹ̀ rẹ̀ gẹ́gẹ́ bí ogún. / When their father died, his children shared out his house and land as their inheritance.
  - Doubt: Tones from Wiktionary. The same spelling also means twenty, and ogun (no mark) is war. Sentence drafted by the model: dividing a dead father's property singles out inheritance; confirm pín (share out) and gẹ́gẹ́ bí (as).

#### Idioms (`m-idioms`)
Sources consulted: Web search for fàáké kọ́rí and fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ found no source defining either; fàáké kọ́rí was dropped for that reason. headteacher.ng (lesson note on àkànlò èdè) and discoveryoruba.com idiom lists were read; neither lists the ten idioms below.; The model's own knowledge for all ten idioms, their literal and real meanings, and all ten example sentences. None was confirmed in a dictionary.

- `m-idioms-inu-bi`: **inú bí** (to get angry, phrase)
  - Sentence: Inú bí bàbá mi gidigidi nígbà tí mo fọ́ fèrèsé rẹ̀, ó sì pariwo mọ́ mi. / My father got very angry when I broke his window, and he shouted at me.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone becomes angry (the angry person follows: inú bí i, he got angry). Literal: the inside gave birth (or the inside was provoked). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìbínú (anger) appears to come from it. Sentence drafted by the model: shouting after a broken window singles out anger.
- `m-idioms-oju-ti`: **ojú tì** (to feel ashamed, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ojú tì í gan-an nígbà tí wọ́n mú un pé ó ń purọ́, kò lè wo ẹnikẹ́ni lójú. / He felt very ashamed when they caught him lying; he could not look anyone in the face.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone feels ashamed (ojú tì mí, I am ashamed). Literal: the eye (face) closed. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìtìjú (shame) appears related. Sentence drafted by the model: not being able to look at people singles out shame, not anger; confirm mú un pé (caught him that).
- `m-idioms-fese-fe`: **fẹsẹ̀ fẹ** (to flee, take to one's heels, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí olè náà rí ọlọ́pàá, ó fẹsẹ̀ fẹ kí wọ́n má bàa mú un. / When the thief saw the police, he took to his heels so that they would not catch him.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to run away. Literal: to fan (or widen) with the legs (fi ẹsẹ̀ fẹ). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; confirm the tones and the literal reading. Sentence drafted by the model: a thief escaping the police singles out fleeing.
- `m-idioms-foju-di`: **fojú di** (to underestimate, look down on, phrase)
  - Sentence: Má fojú di ọmọdé náà; bí ó tilẹ̀ kéré, ó lè ṣẹ́gun rẹ nínú ayò. / Do not underestimate that child; although he is small, he can beat you at ayò.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to belittle or underrate someone. Literal: to block (someone) with the eye (fi ojú di); the model is unsure of the literal reading. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; the exact form is doubtful (fojú di, fi ojú di, or with an object between), so confirm it. Sentence drafted by the model: small but able to win singles out underestimating; ayò is the Yoruba board game.
- `m-idioms-feti-sile`: **fetí sílẹ̀** (to listen carefully, phrase)
  - Sentence: Ẹ fetí sílẹ̀ dáadáa sí ohun tí olùkọ́ ń sọ, kí ẹ lè dáhùn ìbéèrè rẹ̀. / Listen carefully to what the teacher is saying, so that you can answer his questions.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to listen, pay attention. Literal: to put the ear down (fi etí sílẹ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary. Sentence drafted by the model: attending to a speaker in order to answer singles out listening.
- `m-idioms-gbenu-so`: **gbẹnu sọ** (to speak on behalf of, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nítorí pé àwọn ará abúlé kò lè lọ sí ìpàdé náà, olóyè wọn ló gbẹnu sọ fún wọn. / Because the villagers could not go to the meeting, their chief spoke on their behalf.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to be someone's spokesperson (agbẹnusọ, spokesperson, is from it). Literal: to take up the mouth and speak (gbé ẹnu sọ). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; confirm whether it is written gbẹnusọ as one word. Sentence drafted by the model: speaking for people who are absent singles out this idiom.
- `m-idioms-oju-n-pon`: **ojú ń pọ́n** (to be suffering hardship, phrase)
  - Sentence: Láti ìgbà tí ó ti pàdánù iṣẹ́ rẹ̀, ojú ń pọ́n ọn; kò rí owó ra oúnjẹ. / Since he lost his job he has been suffering hardship; he cannot find money to buy food.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: someone is going through hardship (ojú ń pọ́n mi, I am suffering). Literal: the eye is turning red. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; ìpọ́njú (hardship) appears related. Sentence drafted by the model: losing a job and having no money for food singles out hardship.
- `m-idioms-fokan-bale`: **fọkàn balẹ̀** (to rest assured, be at ease, phrase)
  - Sentence: Fọkàn balẹ̀, ọmọ rẹ ti dé láìléwu. / Rest assured, your child has arrived safely.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: stop worrying, be calm. Literal: let the heart land (fi ọkàn balẹ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary. Sentence drafted by the model: news that a child arrived safely singles out reassurance; confirm láìléwu (safely).
- `m-idioms-erin-wo`: **erin wó** (a great person has died, phrase)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí ọba ìlú wa kú, àwọn ará ìlú ń sọ pé erin wó. / When the king of our town died, the townspeople were saying that a great person had died.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: a king or great person has died. Literal: the elephant has fallen. From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; it is said to be used out of respect instead of saying kú (die) of a great person. Sentence drafted by the model: the death of a king singles out this idiom.
- `m-idioms-foro-lo`: **fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀** (to consult, phrase)
  - Sentence: Kí ó tó ra ilé náà, ó fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ bàbá rẹ̀ láti gbọ́ èrò rẹ̀. / Before he bought the house, he consulted his father to hear his opinion.
  - Doubt: Real meaning: to ask someone's advice. Literal: to grind a matter with someone (fi ọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀). From the model's own knowledge, not confirmed in a dictionary; a web search found no source. The exact form is doubtful (fọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀ or the unjoined fi ọ̀rọ̀ lọ̀), and so is whether it is common. Confirm, or replace it. Sentence drafted by the model: asking a father's opinion before buying singles out consulting.

#### Words from the proverbs (`m-proverbs`)
Sources consulted: src/content/lessons/m-4.ts: the proverbs Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà (Afolayan 2021) and Òní l'a rí, ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la (Wiktionary "oni", citing the Global Yoruba Lexical Database); kọ́ (teach, build), gbé, ọba, ọba òkè (the Most High), and pa òwe (COERLL p. 262). Same spellings. Not rechecked for this topic.; src/content/lessons/m-2.ts: the saying Ìmọ́tótó ló lè ṣẹ́gun àrùn gbogbo (COERLL), source of ìmọ́tótó, ṣẹ́gun and àrùn. Same spellings. Not rechecked for this topic.; The model's own knowledge for òwe alone, kọ́lé, and all ten example sentences (new sentences, not the proverbs).

- `m-proverbs-owe`: **òwe** (proverb, noun)
  - Sentence: Òwe ni ọ̀rọ̀ kúkúrú tí àwọn àgbà fi ń kọ́ni ní ọgbọ́n. / A proverb is a short saying that elders use to teach wisdom.
  - Doubt: As in the Mastery 4 lesson title. Sentence drafted by the model: a definition, so no other word fits; confirm kọ́ni (teach people).
- `m-proverbs-pa-owe`: **pa òwe** (cite a proverb, verb)
  - Sentence: Nígbà tí àgbà náà fẹ́ gbà wá nímọ̀ràn, ó pa òwe kan fún wa. / When the elder wanted to advise us, he cited a proverb for us.
  - Doubt: COERLL (quoted in the Mastery 4 lesson sources) gives pa òwe, to say proverbially. Sentence drafted by the model: an elder giving advice singles out citing a proverb; confirm gbà ... nímọ̀ràn (advise).
- `m-proverbs-ko`: **kọ́** (teach, verb)
  - Sentence: Ìyá mi ló kọ́ mi bí a ṣe ń se ọbẹ̀ ẹ̀gúsí. / It was my mother who taught me how to cook egusi soup.
  - Doubt: The teach sense of kọ́ from the first Mastery 4 proverb (the child we did not teach). Sentence drafted by the model: showing someone how to cook singles out teach, not build or carry. Confirm ló (ni ó) and se ọbẹ̀ (cook soup).
- `m-proverbs-kole`: **kọ́lé** (build a house, verb)
  - Sentence: Bàbá mi fẹ́ kọ́lé tirẹ̀, nítorí náà ó ra bíríkì àti sìmẹ́ǹtì. / My father wants to build his own house, so he bought bricks and cement.
  - Doubt: The model's own knowledge: kọ́ (build) + ilé (house) joined, the build sense in the same proverb (the house we built). Confirm the form. Sentence drafted by the model: bricks and cement single out building; confirm the loanwords bíríkì and sìmẹ́ǹtì.
- `m-proverbs-gbe`: **gbé** (carry, verb)
  - Sentence: Ó gbé ẹrù wúwo náà lórí lọ sí ọjà. / She carried the heavy load on her head to the market.
  - Doubt: From the first proverb (gbé ... tà, take and sell) and Mastery 2 (gbé igi, carry the stick). gbé also means live (dwell). Sentence drafted by the model: a load on the head singles out carry; confirm lórí (on the head).
- `m-proverbs-oba`: **ọba** (king, noun)
  - Sentence: Ọba ìlú wa ń gbé ní ààfin, adé sì wà ní orí rẹ̀. / The king of our town lives in the palace, and a crown is on his head.
  - Doubt: As taught in Intermediate 2 and used in the second Mastery 4 proverb. Sentence drafted by the model: a palace and a crown single out king, not the Most High; confirm ààfin (palace) and adé (crown).
- `m-proverbs-oba-oke`: **ọba òkè** (the Most High (God), noun)
  - Sentence: Kò sí ẹni tí ó mọ ohun tí ọ̀la yóò mú wá àfi ọba òkè, ẹni tí ó dá ayé. / No one knows what tomorrow will bring except the Most High, who created the world.
  - Doubt: From the second Mastery 4 proverb, where Wiktionary translates it the Most High (literally king above). Sentence drafted by the model: knowing tomorrow and creating the world single it out, echoing the proverb without copying it; an earthly king does not fit. Confirm àfi (except).
- `m-proverbs-imototo`: **ìmọ́tótó** (cleanliness, noun)
  - Sentence: Fífọ ọwọ́, gbígbá ilé àti wíwẹ̀ déédéé jẹ́ ara ìmọ́tótó. / Washing hands, sweeping the house and bathing regularly are part of cleanliness.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2 (a health saying rather than a traditional proverb). Sentence drafted by the model: washing, sweeping and bathing single out cleanliness; confirm the verbal nouns fífọ, gbígbá and wíwẹ̀.
- `m-proverbs-segun`: **ṣẹ́gun** (defeat, overcome, verb)
  - Sentence: Ẹgbẹ́ wa ṣẹ́gun ẹgbẹ́ wọn nínú ìdíje bọ́ọ̀lù, a sì gba ife ẹ̀yẹ. / Our team defeated their team in the football match, and we won the trophy.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2 (cleanliness can overcome all disease). Sentence drafted by the model: winning a match and a trophy singles out defeat; confirm ìdíje (competition) and ife ẹ̀yẹ (trophy).
- `m-proverbs-arun`: **àrùn** (disease, noun)
  - Sentence: Àrùn yìí máa ń ràn láti ara ẹnì kan sí ẹlòmíràn, nítorí náà wọ́n ya àwọn aláìsàn sọ́tọ̀. / This disease spreads from one person to another, so they kept the sick apart.
  - Doubt: From the COERLL saying in Mastery 2. Sentence drafted by the model: spreading between people singles out disease; confirm ràn (spread, infect) and ya ... sọ́tọ̀ (separate).

## Sound Bank

- `v-a`: **a** (vowels): like 'a' in 'father'
  - Examples: àga (chair), ajá (dog), àná (yesterday)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-e`: **e** (vowels): like 'e' in 'they'
  - Examples: erin (elephant), ejò (snake), etí (ear)
  - Doubt: Check the comparison; Yoruba e is a pure vowel.
- `v-ee`: **ẹ** (vowels): like 'e' in 'bed'
  - Examples: ẹja (fish), ẹran (meat), ẹyin (egg)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-i`: **i** (vowels): like 'ee' in 'see', shorter
  - Examples: ilé (house), irun (hair), ikùn (stomach)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `v-o`: **o** (vowels): like 'o' in 'go', without the glide
  - Examples: omi (water), orí (head), ojú (eye)
  - Doubt: Check the comparison; Yoruba o is a pure vowel.
- `v-oo`: **ọ** (vowels): like 'aw' in 'saw'
  - Examples: ọmọ (child), ọkọ (husband), ọwọ́ (hand)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate (varies by accent).
- `v-u`: **u** (vowels): like 'oo' in 'moon', shorter
  - Examples: iṣu (yam), ẹnu (mouth), ìlú (town)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `n-an`: **an** (vowels): a nasal 'a', like the vowel in French 'blanc'
  - Examples: ọkàn (heart), ẹran (meat)
  - Doubt: Nasal vowel; check example words and the French comparison. In Standard Yoruba an is often said like ọn, so the French comparison is approximate.
- `n-en`: **ẹn** (vowels): a nasal 'ẹ', like the vowel in French 'vin'
  - Examples: ìyẹn (that one), yẹn (that, as in ilé yẹn)
  - Doubt: Rare and debated nasal vowel; example words are my least certain, a native speaker must confirm or replace them.
- `n-in`: **in** (vowels): a nasal 'i', like English 'ee' said through the nose
  - Examples: erin (elephant), mẹ́rin (four)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `n-un`: **un** (vowels): a nasal 'u', like English 'oo' said through the nose
  - Examples: irun (hair), ikùn (stomach)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `n-on`: **ọn** (vowels): a nasal 'ọ', like the vowel in French 'bon'
  - Examples: ẹ̀gbọ́n (older sibling), ìbọn (gun)
  - Doubt: Check comparison and example words.
- `c-b`: **b** (consonants): like 'b' in 'bat'
  - Examples: bàbá (father), bàtà (shoe), ọ̀bọ (monkey)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-d`: **d** (consonants): like 'd' in 'dog'
  - Examples: dúdú (black), adìyẹ (chicken), ọdún (year)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-f`: **f** (consonants): like 'f' in 'fun'
  - Examples: funfun (white), fìlà (cap), fèrèsé (window)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-g`: **g** (consonants): like 'g' in 'go'
  - Examples: gèlè (head wrap), àga (chair)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-gb`: **gb** (consonants): one sound made by saying 'g' and 'b' together, lips closed
  - Examples: agbádá (agbada robe), ẹ̀gbọ́n (older sibling), ológbò (cat)
  - Doubt: Single sound, not g then b. Check the description and examples.
- `c-h`: **h** (consonants): like 'h' in 'hat'
  - Examples: ahọ́n (tongue), ihò (hole)
  - Doubt: Check example words; h is not common.
- `c-j`: **j** (consonants): like 'j' in 'jam'
  - Examples: ejò (snake), ọjọ́ (day)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-k`: **k** (consonants): like 'k' in 'kit'
  - Examples: kọ́kọ́rọ́ (key), ikùn (stomach), ọkọ (husband)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-l`: **l** (consonants): like 'l' in 'lamp'
  - Examples: ilé (house), ọ̀la (tomorrow), alẹ́ (night)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-m`: **m** (consonants): like 'm' in 'man'
  - Examples: omi (water), méjì (two), ọmọ (child)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-n`: **n** (consonants): like 'n' in 'net'
  - Examples: ẹnu (mouth), òní (today), àná (yesterday)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-p`: **p** (consonants): written p but said like 'kp': 'k' and 'p' together, lips closed
  - Examples: pupa (red), àpò (bag)
  - Doubt: Yoruba p is a kp sound; check description and examples.
- `c-r`: **r** (consonants): a quick tap of the tongue, like the 'tt' in American 'butter'
  - Examples: erin (elephant), ọ̀rẹ́ (friend)
  - Doubt: English comparison approximate.
- `c-s`: **s** (consonants): like 's' in 'sun'
  - Examples: sùn (sleep), èso (fruit), ọ̀sẹ̀ (week)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-sh`: **ṣ** (consonants): like 'sh' in 'ship'
  - Examples: aṣọ (cloth), ṣòkòtò (trousers), iṣu (yam)
  - Doubt: Check; do not confuse with s.
- `c-t`: **t** (consonants): like 't' in 'top'
  - Examples: tábìlì (table), etí (ear), mẹ́ta (three)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-w`: **w** (consonants): like 'w' in 'water'
  - Examples: ọwọ́ (hand), ẹ̀wù (garment), wàrà (milk)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `c-y`: **y** (consonants): like 'y' in 'yes'
  - Examples: ẹyin (egg), yàrá (room), ìyá (mother)
  - Doubt: Plain consonant.
- `p-oko-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, three tones, three meanings.
  - Words: ọkọ (husband), ọkọ̀ (vehicle), ọkọ́ (hoe)
  - Doubt: Check all three meanings and tones.
- `p-igba-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, three tones, three meanings.
  - Words: igba (two hundred), ìgbà (time), igbá (calabash)
  - Doubt: Native speaker to check the tones of all three words (igba, ìgbà, igbá) and their meanings.
- `p-eyin-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Same letters, different tones and vowel marks.
  - Words: ẹyin (egg), eyín (tooth), ẹ̀yìn (back)
  - Doubt: eyín tone is a known doubt.
- `p-apa-tone`: minimal pair (tone): Only the tone changes.
  - Words: apá (arm), àpá (scar)
  - Doubt: Check meanings and tones.
- `p-oko-sound`: minimal pair (sound): o and ọ are different vowels.
  - Words: oko (farm), ọkọ (husband)
  - Doubt: Check meanings.
- `p-owo-sound`: minimal pair (sound): o and ọ are different vowels.
  - Words: owó (money), ọwọ́ (hand)
  - Doubt: Check meanings and tones.
- `p-ose-sound`: minimal pair (sound): s and ṣ are different sounds (tones differ too).
  - Words: ọ̀sẹ̀ (week), ọṣẹ (soap)
  - Doubt: Tones also differ, so not a pure sound pair; check.
- `tc-a`: tone comparison for **a**
  - Words (mid, high, low): wa (our), wá (come), wà (be, be located)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "wa" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Check all three tones and meanings.
- `tc-e`: tone comparison for **e**
  - Words (mid, high, low): le (hard), lé (chase), lè (can)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "le" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The mid word le has several unrelated meanings on its page; check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-ee`: tone comparison for **ẹ**
  - Words (mid, high, low): bẹ (peel), bẹ́ (hop down), bẹ̀ (beg)
  - Source: Wiktionary, Yoruba section of the page "bẹ" (en.wiktionary.org/wiki/<page>), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: The mid word bẹ has several unrelated meanings on its page; check the mid tone and the chosen meaning.
- `tc-i`: tone comparison for **i**
  - Words (mid, high, low): ṣi (make a mistake), ṣí (open), ṣì (become dull)

## Proverbs

- `aaro-ki-i-gbona`: **Ààrò kì í gbóná kalẹ́.**
  - Word for word: The hearth does not stay hot until nighttime.
  - Meaning: Hard times do not last forever.
  - Wrong options: Cooking should be finished before dark. / A fire needs constant tending.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aaro ki i gbona kalẹ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary also lists the alternative form àdìrò kì í gbóná kalẹ́. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `aisi-nle-ekun`: **Àìsí-ńlé ẹkùn, ajá ń gbó.**
  - Word for word: In the leopard's absence from home, the dog barks.
  - Meaning: When the person in charge is away, others misbehave or grow bold.
  - Wrong options: Dogs are braver than leopards. / Leopards are afraid of dogs.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aisi-nle ẹkun, aja n gbo" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists a variant, àìsí-ńlé ológbò, ilé dilé èkúté. Confirm the tones and the hyphen. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `aja-ki-i-roro`: **Ajá kì í rorò kó ṣọ́ ojúlé méjì.**
  - Word for word: A dog is never so fierce that it can guard two doorways.
  - Meaning: No one can serve two masters or give full attention to two demands at once.
  - Wrong options: Fierce dogs make the best guards. / Two houses are safer than one.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "aja ki i roro ko ṣọ ojule meji" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Confirm the tones, especially kó ṣọ́. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ajo-ki-i-dun`: **Àjò kì í dùn kónílé má relé.**
  - Word for word: A journey cannot be so good that the homeowner does not return home.
  - Meaning: However well things go away from home, a person should not forget where they come from.
  - Wrong options: Travel is not worth the cost. / A house is only a place to sleep.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ajo ki i dun konile ma rele" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary also gives àjò ò lè dùn kónílé má relé as an alternative form. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `agbada-o-ba-gbona`: **Bí agbada ò bá gbóná, àgbàdo ò lè ta.**
  - Word for word: If the pan is not hot, the corn will not pop.
  - Meaning: You cannot get a result without the effort or the cost it takes.
  - Wrong options: Cook corn in a hot pan only on market days. / A pan should be cleaned after every meal.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi agbada o ba gbona, agbado o le ta" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists two alternative forms (tí agbada ò bá gbóná... and bí agbada kò bá gbóná...). Confirm which wording speakers use. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ewe-ba-pe-lara-ose`: **Bí ewé bá pẹ́ lára ọṣẹ, á dọṣẹ.**
  - Word for word: If a leaf stays on the soap long enough, it will become soap.
  - Meaning: People who spend a long time together begin to take on each other's ways.
  - Wrong options: Leaves wash clean when they touch soap. / Soap should be kept away from plants.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi ewe ba pẹ lara ọṣẹ, a dọṣẹ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense says the leaf is wrapped around the soap. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `majala-a-fo-wa-sile`: **Bí iná bá jó lóko, màjàlà á fò wá sílé.**
  - Word for word: When a fire burns in the field, the soot flies home.
  - Meaning: News of what happens far away always finds its way back home.
  - Wrong options: Fires on the farm should be put out quickly. / Soot is the sign of a good cook.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "bi ina ba jo loko, majala a fo wa sile" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists a second form ending ṣòfófó (the soot gossips). Confirm which form is standard and the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `epe-la-fi-n-wo-epe`: **Èpè la fi ń wo èpè sàn.**
  - Word for word: Curses are what we use to cure curses.
  - Meaning: The remedy has to match the trouble. You answer like with like.
  - Wrong options: Never answer an insult. / Words cannot hurt anyone.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "epe la fi n wo epe san" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary glosses this as fighting fire with fire. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ise-enia-nise-eranko`: **Ìṣe ènìyàn nìṣe ẹranko.**
  - Word for word: The actions of humans are the actions of animals.
  - Meaning: People behave much as animals do, so watching animals teaches you about people.
  - Wrong options: Humans and animals should never be kept together. / Animals are wiser than people.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "iṣe eniyan niṣe ẹranko" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists several alternative spellings (ìṣe èèyàn nìṣe ẹranko, ìṣe ènìyàn ni ìṣe ẹranko). Confirm the standard form. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ka-rin-ka-po`: **Ká rìn ká pọ̀, yíyẹ ní ń yẹni.**
  - Word for word: Let us walk together; being fitting is what suits a person.
  - Meaning: People look their best when they live and work together, not alone.
  - Wrong options: Walking alone is faster. / Travelling in a group is slower.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ka rin ka pọ, yiyẹ ni n yẹni" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense reads: traveling in the company of others shows people in a good light. The word-for-word line here is the author's, so confirm it. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `omi-tuntun-ti-ru`: **Omi tuntun ti rú, ẹja tuntun ti wọ̀ ọ́.**
  - Word for word: New water has stirred, new fish have entered it.
  - Meaning: Every era brings its own fashions and its own people.
  - Wrong options: Fish cannot live in new water. / Rivers should be left undisturbed.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "omi tuntun ti ru, ẹja tuntun ti wọ ọ" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists the alternative forms omi tuntun ti dé, ẹja tuntun ti wọ̀ ọ́ and omi titun dé, ẹja titun wọ̀ ọ́. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `obe-to-dun`: **Ọbẹ̀ tó dùn, owó ló pa á.**
  - Word for word: A delicious soup, it is money that made it.
  - Meaning: Nothing good comes without money.
  - Wrong options: Soup tastes best when it is shared. / Money should never be spent on food.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ọbẹ to dun, owo lo pa a" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary's literal sense reads: delicious soups are procured with money. The word-for-word line here is the author's, so confirm it. The verb pa (literally kill, strike) is read here in its idiom of making or procuring, so the word-for-word line says made. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `ona-kan-o-woja`: **Ọ̀nà kan ò wọjà.**
  - Word for word: One road alone does not enter the market.
  - Meaning: There is more than one way to reach a goal.
  - Wrong options: The market is best reached by the shortest road. / Roads to the market are always crowded.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ọna kan o wọja" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary lists the alternative form ọ̀nà kan ò wọ ọjà. Confirm the tones, and note that the English line follows Wiktionary's literal sense: it is not one road only that leads to the market. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `sago-n-bugo`: **Ṣágo ń búgò.**
  - Word for word: The demijohn insults the bottle.
  - Meaning: Someone with the same fault is criticising another for it.
  - Wrong options: Whoever is bigger should be obeyed. / Bottles are worth more than demijohns.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "ṣago n bugo" (Yoruba section), read 2026-10-19
  - Doubt: Wiktionary glosses this as pot calling the kettle black. Confirm the tones. The "used when" line is the author's reading of Wiktionary's figurative sense.
- `omo-ti-a-ko-ko-proverb`: **Ọmọ tí a kò kọ́ ni yóò gbé ilé tí a kọ́ tà.**
  - Word for word: The child we did not teach is the one who will carry the house we built and sell it.
  - Meaning: A child who is not raised and taught well will waste what their parents worked to build.
  - Wrong options: Children should sell the house their parents built. / Teaching a child costs more than building a house.
  - Source: Afolayan, "Ọmọ Tí A Kò Kọ́: Globalization and Cultural Education among New Generation Nigerian Yorùbá", Yoruba Studies Review 3(1), 2021, quoted in lesson Mastery 4; translated "the child that is not taught will eventually sell the house that is built"
  - Doubt: Very high doubt: wording found in one source only, so confirm every tone. The social meaning is the author's reading of that translation. The proverb plays on kọ́ (teach) and kọ́ (build). The literal line is the author's.
- `oni-la-ri`: **Òní l'a rí, ọba òkè l'ó rọ́la.**
  - Word for word: Today is what we see; the King above (God) is the one who sees tomorrow.
  - Meaning: Only God knows what tomorrow brings, so no one can be sure of the future.
  - Wrong options: Wealth should be saved for tomorrow. / Rich people can buy a look at tomorrow.
  - Source: Wiktionary, "oni" (Yoruba section), quoting Awoyale, Global Yoruba Lexical Database v. 1.0 (Linguistic Data Consortium, 2008), translated "It is [only] today that we see, [only] the Most High sees tomorrow" and called a proverb on divine supremacy; quoted in lesson Mastery 4
  - Doubt: Very high doubt: one source. Confirm the wording, the apostrophe spelling (l' read as ni) and the tones. The social meaning follows the source's 'divine supremacy' label; the literal line and the used-when line are the author's.

## Transformations

- `neg-mo-lo` (negative): **Mo lọ** (I went)
  - Accepted answers: N kò lọ | N ò lọ | Mi ò lọ
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. Before kò, mo becomes n (n kò, or n ò for short). Mi ò is the everyday form.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Model answer follows lesson b-2. Confirm n kò versus mi ò as the usual form, and the reading "did not go" versus "am not going".
- `q-o-fe-jeun` (question): **O fẹ́ jẹun** (You want to eat)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement. Nothing else moves.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: The question is written in COERLL and the lessons. Tones are from Wiktionary and an encoding-damaged PDF, so confirm them.
- `fut-ade-jeun` (future): **Adé jẹun** (Adé ate)
  - Accepted answers: Adé máa jẹun | Adé yóò jẹun
  - Rule: Put máa (or yóò) before the verb to talk about what is going to happen.
  - Source: Wiktionary, Template "yo-aspects" (Ajani 2001): máa anticipative and yóò intentional; examples Ẹ̀yin yóò wá kí wa lọ́la, Èmi yóò máa lọ, A ti máa lọ kí o tó dé. Wiktionary, "rọ": Òjò máa rọ̀ (it will rain).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Wiktionary shows yóò mostly with pronouns, so confirm it with a name as subject.
- `past-mo-jeun` (past): **Mo ń jẹun** (I am eating)
  - Accepted answers: Mo jẹun
  - Rule: Drop ń. The bare verb means the action is done.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 3, page 38 (without ń the verb means past: Mò ń lọ, Mo lọ). Wiktionary, "n" (Mo ń jẹun) and Template "yo-aspects" (unmarked verb is completed: mo lọ sí ilé-ìwé, I went to school; progressive Wọ́n ń ṣiṣẹ́).
  - Doubt: COERLL writes the pronoun with a low tone before ń (Mò ń jẹun), Wiktionary writes Mo. Confirm the reading "I ate".
- `neg-o-lo` (negative): **O lọ** (You went)
  - Accepted answers: O ò lọ | O kò lọ
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. After o it is usually shortened to ò.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 2, page 35 (kò goes before the verb) and Chapter 3, Lesson 2, page 76 (pronoun table: N kò, and kò alone for he or she). Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Confirm the tone on the shortened form after o.
- `q-o-sun` (question): **O sùn dáadáa** (You slept well)
  - Accepted answers: Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?
  - Rule: Put ṣé in front of the statement to ask yes or no.
  - Source: COERLL, Yorùbá Yé Mi, Chapter 1, Lesson 4, page 41 (Ṣé o fẹ́ jẹun?) and the Lesson 1 dialogue, page 27 (Ṣé o sùn dáadáa?). Wiktionary, "ṣe" (ṣé before a statement makes a yes or no question) and "daadaa" (Ṣé ẹ sùn dáadáa?).
  - Doubt: The question is written in the COERLL dialogue. Confirm the tones on sùn and dáadáa.
- `fut-oja-ro` (future): **Òjò rọ̀** (It rained)

  - Rule: Put máa (or yóò) before the verb for what will happen.

  - Accepted answers: Adé kò sùn
  - Rule: kò goes before the verb. After a name it stays kò.

Wiktionary, "ko" and "o" (ò is a short form of kò after personal pronouns), "mi" and "ṣe" (Mi ò fẹ́ ṣe nǹkan kan, I don't want to do anything).
  - Doubt: The sentence is a new example. Confirm that a name before kò is not shortened.

  - Example on its own: Ọ̀kan, èjì, ẹ̀ta. (One, two, three.)

The example on its own was drafted by the model from the counting forms (Wikipedia lists them for counting and sums); confirm speakers use the counting forms there.

## Songs

- `okunkun`: **Okunkun** by Solana & Killertunes (Pop)
  - Ọjọ́ ti fe ṣ'òkùnkùn, ẹmi nìkan péré (The day is about to turn dark, it is only me.)
  - Mi o duro, kia-kia mo múra mo mere (I am not standing still; quickly I get ready and go.)
  - Ohun búburú ninu òkùnkùn lo ma n da wa (It is bad things in the darkness that will trouble us.)
  - Ohun k'ohun ti mo sọ emi duro lori rẹ (Whatever I say, I stand on it.)
  - Ẹlẹ́dàá mi gbé mi s'òkè, òkè gidi gan (My Creator lifted me up, really high up.)
  - Were, were, kia-kia wa je ka ma lo (Quickly, quickly, come, let us go.)
  - Ọjọ́ ti fé ṣ'òkùnkùn baba wa e gbá wa (The day is about to turn dark, our father, hold on to us.)
  - A fe sere, sere, lo, lo, ma lo sọnù (We want to hurry, hurry, go, go, do not get lost.)
